# COMPETITIVE BUNDLING IN INFORMATION MARKETS: A SELLER-SIDE ANALYSIS<sup>1</sup>

Srinivasan Raghunathan and Sumit Sarkar Naveen Jindal School of Management, The University of Texas at Dallas, Richardson, TX 75080 U.S.A. {sraghu@utdallas.edu} {sumit@utdallas.edu}

The emerging field of data analytics and the increasing importance of data and information in decision making has created a large market for buying and selling information and information-related services. In this market, for some types of information products, it is common for a consumer to purchase the same type of information product from multiple sources. In other situations, a consumer may buy different types of information products from different sources and synthesize the information. On the seller side, bundling of different types of information products appears to have emerged as a key design strategy to improve profitability. This paper examines bundling decisions of a duopoly in the information market in which each seller offers two (or more) types of information products. A pair of competing information products from the two sellers can be substitutes or complements and consumers may find it profitable to purchase the same type of information from both sellers. We show that bundling by both sellers emerges as the equilibrium outcome when (at least) one competing pair consists of substitutes and (at least) one pair consists of complements. In this case, bundling by both sellers benefits them both by softening the price competition between their offerings. Softening of competition does not occur when all competing pairs in the bundles have only substitutes (complements) even if the degree of substitutability (complementarity) between products within a pair varies across pairs, resulting in an equilibrium in which each information type is sold separately by both sellers.

Keywords: Bundling, pricing, competition, analytical models, information markets

## Introduction

The increasing importance of data and information in decision making has created a large market for selling and buying information and information-related services. The emerging data analytics field is also contributing to the growth of information markets. McKinsey & Company reported that the business-to-business information market alone was more than \$200 billion in 2008 and was growing between 6 percent and 8 percent annually (Bensen-Armer and Hasker 2009). Information that is sold takes a variety of forms such as industry reports, databases, expert opinions, and advice. Informationrelated services include, among others, consulting, information exchange services, and trading services on exchanges. Decision makers in a wide range of industries such as finance, consumer goods, health, law, and scientific research are the typical consumers of information products and services.

In information markets, the primary objective of a buyer or consumer of information is to obtain a reliable estimate of some quantity of interest that will help her make a sound decision. It is common in these markets for a consumer to purchase certain types of information from multiple sources before making a decision. For example, financial institutions often purchase the credit score and credit report of a loan applicant from multiple credit bureaus. Institutional investors use multiple brokerage houses to help with their investment decision making. Sarvary (2002) and Ozdemir et al. (2006) provide several examples of industries where a market for second opinion is prevalent. In some contexts, a consumer may also purchase different types of information from different sources and synthesize them before making a decision (Padmanabhan et al. 2006). For instance, a retailer may purchase demographic data from one source and web-usage behavior from another to decide on its marketing strategy.

On the seller side, a seller in these markets typically offers a variety of information ranging from raw data to sophisticated insights and advice derived from such data that help buyers get maximum value from the information. In the credit information market, for example, while an individual’s credit history can be viewed as the raw data, the proprietary credit score provides a more sophisticated insight about the creditworthiness of the individual. Some of the key questions faced by sellers in information markets are how to “design” their product offering and how to price it. Although the information market is a significant part of the overall economy, research on the above key questions is limited. On the other hand, the rapidly growing data analytics field is likely to fuel the need for answers to product design and pricing related questions.

Anecdotal observations in information markets suggest that bundling of information products seems to have emerged as a key design strategy to improve sellers’ profitability. For instance, in the financial brokerage industry, most firms bundle a variety of information ranging from stock history, analysis, and investment advice to market opinion, which are collectively referred to as research, and trading services that include providing access to real-time stock information such as quote, volume, and bid/ask prices, which are referred to as execution. Referring to the low degree of differentiation between products offered by discount and full service brokerage firms, Sloan (1999) noted that “Merrill getting more like E\*Trade, and E\*Trade getting more like Merrill, is what’s called convergence.” The traditional full service firms such as Merrill Lynch that had been claiming that “execution is a commodity but our real product is advice” have switched to a strategy that offers both discount trading and advice, and discount traders such as Schwab offer proprietary advice along with trading. In the marketing research sector, AC Nielsen and IRI offer packaged solutions that include the raw marketing research data and modeling tools to extract intelligence from these data.

There is extensive literature on product bundling in physical goods markets. Several researchers have also examined bundling of digital goods that have negligible marginal production cost such as software. The products we consider in this paper also have negligible marginal production cost, but are different from digital products such as software in that the information products we consider are mostly numerical or categorical estimates of some quantity in which the consumer is interested. Therefore, concepts such as accuracy, mean, variance, and correlation are inherent characteristics of the information products we consider, but these concepts are not the essential features of digital goods considered by prior research. We could not find research on product bundling in markets for information products of this nature.Prior literature in other product markets has shown that bundling can be an effective price discrimination tool for a monopolist and an effective product differentiation tool or a leverage mechanism for a firm under competition. The existing theories of bundling generally predict intensified competition when competing sellers bundle their products (Bakos and Brynjolfsson 2000; Chen 1997).<sup>2</sup> However, the ubiquitous nature of bundling in the information product markets seems to run counter to that prediction.In this paper, we analyze the bundling decisions of a duopoly in the information market in which each seller offers two (or more) types of information and consumers may find it profitable to purchase the same type of information from both sellers. We show that unique competitive structures associated with information products can explain why bundling by competing sellers may have a different impact in information markets than in others.

The key intuition from our analysis is the following: In information markets, even after a consumer has bought information from one seller to estimate a quantity of interest needed to make her decision, she may find that information from another seller for the same quantity offers a positive incremental value. The incremental value offered by the second information is likely to be high (possibly even more than the value offered by the first information alone) when the reliability of information from a single source is low and the two information sources are independent (Sarvary and Parker 1997). If the incremental utility from buying the second product in a competing pair is larger (smaller) than the utility from buying a single product in the competing pair, then competing products are complements (substitutes). Clearly, competition between sellers is likely to be more intense when the competing products are substitutes than when they are complements. By bundling a product that is a complement to the product in its competing pair with another product that is a substitute to the one in its competing pair, sellers are able to effectively shape the relative incremental value of information to consumers, and hence soften the intensity of competition between sellers. Softening of competition does not occur when both products in the bundle are substitutes with their corresponding products, or when both are complements with their corresponding products. The softening of competition occurs only when products that have opposite competitive structures are bundled. Furthermore, softening of competition occurs only when both sellers bundle. Models that assume consumers buy only one product from a competing set (by effectively assuming that the incremental value from the second product is zero) are unable to exploit the effect of bundling on the shape of the consumer valuation of products. We demonstrate the impact of bundling in information markets discussed above by employing a stylized model that precludes many of the impacts identified by prior bundling literature.<sup>3</sup>

## Related Literature

Venkatesh and Mahajan (2009) provide a comprehensive review of the bundling literature.<sup>4</sup> We discuss only the works that are closely related to our paper. Major theories of product bundling fall into three categories: price discrimination, leverage, and product differentiation. In price discrimination, bundling is a strategy exploited by a multiproduct monopolist to discriminate consumers based on their heterogeneous valuations to extract more of their surplus. Stigler (1963), Adams and Yellen (1976), Schmalensee (1984), and others (McAfee et al. 1989; Salinger 1987) have extensively analyzed this strategy for a two-product bundle. Bakos and Brynjolfsson (1999) extended this theory to bundles with large numbers of products; however, Geng et al. (2005) showed that bundling of a large number of products is optimal only if a consumer’s value does not decline too rapidly with the number of goods consumed.

In leverage theory, bundling enables a seller with monopoly power in one market to leverage its power to deter entry or foreclose sales in another market. In this stream of research (e.g., Carbajo et al. 1990; Nalebuff 2004; Whinston 1990), the bundling firm is a multiproduct firm and the competing firms are single product firms. Nalebuff (2004) also analyzed how a multiproduct firm might use bundling to leverage the complementarity among its products. Parker and Van Alstyne (2005) found that unbundling, rather than bundling, is optimal when network externalities lead firms to design complementary products. In a paper related to leverage theory, but without strategic considerations between sellers, Shaffer (1991) considered a manufacturer selling two substitute products to a retailer. Because the products are substitutes, by selling them separately the manufacturer is able to extract only part of the rent obtained by the retailer. By bundling the products, Shaffer showed that the manufacturer is able to extract the entire rent. Bhargava (2012) considered a model in which competing upstream manufacturers sell through a common retailer and the retailer controls the bundling strategy. He showed that bundling is not the equilibrium outcome.

In product differentiation, bundling is viewed as a strategy used by competing multiproduct sellers to soften price competition by offering differentiated product offerings. For example, Chen (1997) showed that an asymmetric strategy in which one firm sells two products as a bundle and another firm sells only one product helps differentiate the offerings of the two firms. This leads to the firms serving different consumer segments. Bakos and Brynjolfsson (2000) showed that, in a multiproduct duopoly where one firm bundles and the other does not, the bundler acquires several competitive advantages, such as predation and entry deterrence, over the nonbundler. However, when both sellers bundle, each realizes a lower profit than what it would realize if both sell products separately because bundling by both reduces the differentiation between bundles and intensifies the price competition (a prisoners’ dilemma situation). Balachander et al. (2010) showed that a multiproduct firm can use bundling to compete with price promotions offered by a competitor. Eppen et al. (1991) described, through anecdotal examples, other potential reasons for a seller to adopt a bundling strategy, including economies of scale in production, distribution, and promotion activities; improvement in product performance; expansion of market through customer aggregation; and manipulation of switching costs.

Another stream of literature analyzes bundling of complementary products. This literature examines a context in which consumers assemble various components produced by one or more firms into a larger system. The seminal paper in this literature by Matutes and Regibeau (1992) shows that, for a wide range of parameters, the bundling equilibrium has the structure of a prisoner’s dilemma. Anderson and Leruth (1993), using a simulation, show that mixed bundling may be suboptimal, while Economides (1993) shows that mixed bundling is a dominant strategy when demand is linear in price. Farrell et al. (1998) and Liao and Taubman (2002) show that bundling may increase social surplus.

Yet another research stream has studied bundling of products of specialist firms to compete with generalist firms. Denicolo (2000) examined the complementary products market in which there is a generalist firm that produces two components required to assemble a system and there are two other specialist firms each of which produces a component that competes with one of the components of the generalist firm. Denicolo shows that the generalist firm may have an incentive to choose incompatibility or engage in pure bundling when one component is less differentiated than the other. Ghosh and Balachander (2007) analyzed a similar model with a generalist firm and two specialist firms but did not assume the products to be complementary. They show that under some conditions the specialist firms would form a strategic alliance and bundle their products to compete with the generalist firm that would also bundle its products and, further that the equilibrium is not a prisoners’ dilemma.

Our paper is related to Bakos et al. (2005) who analyzed the impact of competition between an incumbent full-service brokerage firm that sells trade execution and research and an entrant discount broker that sells only execution. The key differences between our model and that of Bakos et al. are two-fold. First, we assume that a consumer can buy products from both sellers, which is consistent with observations in the information market, whereas Bakos et al. assumed that a consumer buys from only one of the sellers. Second, consistent with the practice in the brokerage industry of bundling execution and advice, we model simultaneous competition between two firms, each of which offers both types of products, whereas Bakos et al. assumed that the incumbent offers both research and execution and the entrant offers only execution.

Table 1 provides a comparison of our work with seminal studies in prior literature on several key dimensions and illustrates how our work is positioned relative to these other studies. Unlike prior research that assumed heterogeneity of consumer valuations of a product, our model assumes that all consumers have identical valuations. Consequently, reduction in consumer heterogeneity, the primary reason for a monopolist’s bundling strategy and for the prisoner’s dilemma scenario in the case of competing bundles, cannot explain the effect we derive in our paper.<sup>5</sup> Thus, bundling is not a price discrimination tool in our model. Further, because consumers are homogeneous, product differentiation that allows different consumer segments to buy different products/bundles is also infeasible, which rules out that explanation for (or against) a bundling strategy. In our model, neither seller is a monopoly in any of the markets, and products within a bundle are neither required to be substitutes nor complements—hence, the leverage theory of bundling also does not apply. We assume symmetric sellers deciding their strategies simultaneously so that neither firm enjoys an inherent advantage either in the number of products or in the decision timing. Furthermore, we also do not have a generalist firm and specialist firms (both firms in our model produce all products) and, therefore, the strategic considerations related to bundling by specialist firms do not apply in our model. Finally, we do not assume economies of scale or cost reduction from bundling.

Interestingly, our result is in contrast to that of Chen who showed that in equilibrium only one firm will bundle, and that of Bakos and Bynjolfsson (2000) who showed that bundling by both firms reduces their profits. We show that the asymmetric strategy of one firm selling the products as a bundle and the other selling them separately is not an equilibrium, and that bundling of a substitute with a complement by both firms increases their profits. The reason that the asymmetric strategy is not an equilibrium in the problem we study is that when one of the firms (say, Firm A) sells the products separately, the competitive structure does not change even if the other seller (Firm B) bundles its products. This is because the consumer can evaluate the bundle from Firm B against the individual products from Firm A based on the incremental utility of individual products from Firm B. The net effect is that the products in a bundle compete individually with the corresponding products sold separately. On the other hand, when both sellers bundle a substitute with a complement, the competitive structure is altered (i.e., the competition is softened), which benefits both.

Our result that bundling by both sellers is an equilibrium under certain conditions and that this equilibrium is profitable to both sellers is similar to the result shown by Ghosh and Balachander. However, the contexts and drivers are different in the two models. Ghosh and Balachander consider the competition between a generalist firm that sells products in two product categories and two specialist firms each of which sells one product from one of the categories. The competing products in one of the categories are homogenous and in the other are differentiated. Consumers buy at most one product from each category. When the specialist firms bundle their products, they collude to maximize the joint profit, thus directly affecting the nature of competition, from a threeplayer competition in the unbundled case to a two-player competition in the bundled case. In effect, bundling by specialist firms affects not only the consumers’ valuation of products but also the competitive structure in the market. In our model and other models in the bundling literature, bundling does not change the number of players that compete. Furthermore, in Ghosh and Balachander, the increased profitability from bundling comes from an expansion of the market when specialist firms coordinate their offerings and prices. In our model, there is no market expansion because of bundling and there is no strategy coordination of different firms in the bundling equilibrium.

## The Model

We illustrate our model and discuss the findings using examples from the financial brokerage industry. Firms in this industry typically serve institutional clients that manage large investments or retail investors. Brokerage firms that serve institutional investors are referred to as securities firms and those that serve retail investors are referred to as discount or online brokers. Both securities firms and discount brokers offer two types of products to their customers: research and execution (Bakos et al. 2005; Goldstein et al. 2009; Tanaka 2007). Research primarily includes analysis and market insights which help customers make investment decisions by estimating a certain quantity of interest. For instance, a customer could be interested in forecasting the earnings for a company in the next quarter, and different providers may offer different earnings forecasts. On the execution side, brokerage firms provide information to facilitate trades and also mediate between the customer and the stock exchange. For instance, the brokerage firm may provide real-time stock information such as bid/ask prices and quantity, and trading volume to the customer. As with the earnings forecast, the real-time stock information provided by different brokerage houses for the same stock symbol can vary, depending on the quality of the trading platform they use and the exchanges from which they get the data. For example, it is well known that the real time volume data provided by the BATS exchange is lower than the data provided by NASDAQ and NYSE for the same stock.<sup>6</sup> Bakos et al. show that the execution quality (measured using execution time and price improvement) also varies across brokers depending on the exchanges to which brokers route orders. Importantly, the value of information for a consumer depends on the expected error associated with the information (a lower estimation error provides a better value). For expositional convenience, we extend the model of Sarvary and Parker (1997) for a single information product to include the case of multiple products in order to analyze the impact of bundling in the information market.<sup>7</sup>

## Sellers

We consider a symmetric duopoly. Each seller $j , j \in \{ 1 , 2 \}$ sells two information products. For instance, in the financial brokerage industry, one of the products could be research and the other could be execution. The $i ^ { \mathrm { { t h } } }$ product from each seller forms a competing pair, referred to as pair i (i.e., each product in a competing pair is information sold by one of the two sellers for the same quantity of interest). Competition occurs only between products in a pair. We assume information products from different pairs are unrelated; that is, there are no interaction effects among products from different competing pairs.<sup>8</sup> Thus, the unbundled scenario in this setting corresponds to two independent two-good markets. We assume constant marginal as well as fixed production costs. In addition, these costs are assumed to be zero.<sup>9</sup> We keep the base model simple in order to understand and isolate the effect of bundling in information product markets and for expositional clarity. Later, in the “Model Extensions” section, we extend our analysis to consider the impact of asymmetry between competing products, more than two products sold by each seller, positive marginal production cost, and interaction between products sold by a seller.

Analogous to Sarvary and Parker, we assume that the information content of the $i ^ { \mathrm { { t h } } }$ product of the $j ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ seller consists of a number $x _ { i j } ,$ and that $x _ { i j }$ is a random draw from a normal distribution with mean $m _ { i }$ (the true value of the $i ^ { \mathrm { { t h } } }$ information required by consumers) and variance $\sigma _ { i } ^ { 2 }$ , which represents the inverse of the “reliability” of the $i ^ { \mathrm { { t h } } }$ product. That is, the reliability of the two information products in a competing pair is the same. For example, suppose the $i ^ { \mathrm { { t h } } }$ product of sellers is about the earnings estimate for a company, then $m _ { i }$ is the true earnings, and $x _ { i j }$ is the $j ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ seller’s forecast of $m _ { i }$ . $x _ { i j } ^ { \mathrm { ~ ~ } } \mathrm { ~ s ~ }$ provided by the two sellers for a given i need not be independent. We assume that the correlation coefficient between $x _ { i j } ^ { \mathrm { ~ ~ } } \mathrm { ~ s ~ }$ for a specific i is $\rho _ { i }$

The value of $m _ { i }$ is unknown to all players at the time of purchase. We assume the variances and correlation coefficients are common knowledge to sellers and consumers. The assumption is reasonable in many contexts because sellers and consumers have prior experiences with products of a similar nature, and in some cases detailed historical information about such products is available to consumers. For instance, institutional investors assess or score the performance of all brokerage houses they use every quarter or every six months, and the score is an important part of brokerage commissions and fees to be paid in the next period (Tanaka 2007). Such scoring is done separately for both research and execution. Online brokers such as Charles Schwab and publications such as Hulbert Financial Digest provide historical performance data of analysts and brokerage houses for retail investors.

## Consumers

Consumers can choose to buy only one product, from seller 1 or seller 2, or both in a competing pair. If a consumer buys the $i ^ { \mathrm { { t h } } }$ product from firm $j ,$ her estimate of $m _ { i }$ will be $x _ { i j }$ with variance $\boldsymbol { \sigma _ { i } ^ { 2 } } .$ . If a consumer buys the $i ^ { \mathrm { { t h } } }$ product from both sellers, the consumer aggregates the two information products to estimate $m _ { i }$ . Winkler (1981) has derived the probability distribution for the aggregate information and has shown the Bayesian estimate of $m _ { i }$ is with variancex $\textstyle \sum _ { i } ^ { 2 }$ , where

$$
\overline {{x}} _ {i} = \frac {x _ {1 i} + x _ {2 i}}{2}\tag{1}
$$

and

$$
\sum_ {i} ^ {2} = \frac {\sigma_ {i} ^ {2} (1 + \rho_ {i})}{2}\tag{2}
$$

A consumer’s utility depends on the quality of the product(s) she consumes. Following Sarvary and Parker, we define the quality of a product as a linearly decreasing function of the product’s variance with intercept equal to 1, without loss of generality.<sup>10</sup> Recall that a consumer may buy only one product (either from seller 1 or seller 2) in a competing pair, or buy both products. If a consumer buys both products, she aggregates them as discussed above. We refer to the combined set of two products from a competing pair as the aggregate product and denote the aggregate product that results from combining the two products in the $i ^ { \mathrm { { \scriptsize { t h } } } }$ competing pair as . The quality of product i i from a single seller and the quality of the aggregate product consisting of product i from both sellers, is expressed as

$$
\begin{array}{l} s _ {i} = 1 - \sigma_ {i} ^ {2} \text { if   the } i ^ {\text { th }} \text { product   from   only   one   seller   is } \\ \text { bought } \\ s _ {i} = 1 - \Sigma_ {\bar {i}} ^ {2} \text { if   the } i ^ {\text { th }} \text { products   from   both   sellers   are } \\ \text { bought } \end{array}\tag{3}
$$

Thus, the quality and variance of a product are allowed to vary between 0 and 1. We use parameter $k _ { i }$ to capture the marginal consumer utility for product quality. Let the consumer utility from product i be $\nu _ { i } = k _ { i } s _ { i }$ when i is bought from a single seller, and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { { } _ { i } } = k _ { i } s _ { { \scriptscriptstyle i } }$ when i from both sellers are bought.<sup>11</sup> Therefore, the expected surplus to a consumer from buying the $i ^ { \mathrm { { t h } } }$ product, given that sellers charge prices $p _ { i j }$ for their $i ^ { \mathrm { { \overline { { t h } } } } }$ products, is

$$
U _ {i} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \overline {{v}} _ {i} - p _ {i 1} - p _ {i 2} \text {   if   products   from   both   sellers   are   bought } \\ v _ {i} - p _ {i j} \text {   if   product   from   only   seller   j   is   bought } \\ 0 \text {   otherwise } \end{array} \right.\tag{4}
$$

The number of consumers is scaled to 1 without loss of generality. A consumer’s total surplus from buying products from both pairs is the summation of the individual surplus values for products from each pair given by Equation (4) (recall that for our basic model there are no interaction effects among products from different competing pairs).

The following result shows the relationship between the quality of (or valuation from) individual and aggregate products; the relationship has a significant impact on the equilibrium seller prices and profits.<sup>12</sup>

Lemma 1: If then $\rho _ { i } \ge - \Biggl ( \frac { 2 - 3 \sigma _ { i } ^ { 3 } } { \sigma _ { i } ^ { 3 } } \Biggr )$ then $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } \leq 2 \nu _ { i }$ ; otherwise, $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } > 2 \nu _ { i }$

All proofs are provided in Appendix A.

The condition in Lemma 1 can be interpreted as follows: When the reliabilities of products are high, or the products are highly correlated, the quality as a function of the number of competing products bought is concave. In this case, the reservation price for the aggregate information is less than ( )v the sum of individual reservation prices $\left( 2 \nu _ { i } \right)$ . It is intuitive that high reliability or correlation makes information from the second source less valuable. Information from the two sellers is grossly substitutable in this situation. When the reliability is low and information sources are independent, the quality function is convex. Thus, the reservation price for the aggregate information is more than the sum of individual reservation prices.<sup>13</sup> In this case, the information from the two sources complement each other.

Consider research and execution products of the financial brokerage industry. Assume research consists of earnings estimates for the next quarter. Different analysts will likely come up with different estimates, depending on the analysis methods they use, the assumptions they make, and the data they analyze to derive their estimate. If their methods, assumptions, and data vary widely, they are likely to come up with quite different estimates (i.e., the correlation between the different estimates is likely to be low). Furthermore, since this estimate is about the forecast of future earnings and the earnings are affected by variables that are not under the control of the company or the analyst, the estimate is likely to be error-prone. Therefore, such information from different sellers are likely to be complements. On the other hand, execution provides historical or instantaneous raw information rather than a forecast. Although errors are still possible, they may not be marked, and the correlation between information from different providers is likely to be high. Therefore, execution is likely to be a substitute.

Lemma 1 shows that information from two sources can either complement or substitute each other when the two pieces of information are two estimates of the same quantity of interest. The result applies to more general contexts as well. For example, a decision maker may want to predict an unknown variable using data about two or more other variables whose values she can acquire. A specific example could be an online recommendation system that attempts to predict a consumer’s preferred product using the consumer’s demographic data and web-usage behavior. It is easy to see that for any prediction model, two predictor variables may complement or substitute each other depending on the correlation between the two variables (as shown in Appendix B, when two predictor variables are complements or substitutes for a linear prediction model). Since our subsequent results depend primarily on the complementarity or substitutability between information products, the insights derived from this paper apply to a broad class of information product markets in which consumers buy information from multiple sellers for use in their decision making.

Following Brandenburger and Nalebuff (1997) and Venkatesh and Kamakura (2003), we say that products in a competing pair are (competing) complementors if $\overline { { \nu } } _ { { } _ { i } } > 2$ and substitutors if $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } \leq 2$ . For a pair i, we define the degree of substitution, ds<sub>i</sub>, as $2 \nu _ { { _ i } } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { _ i }$ . Thus, if $d s _ { i }$ is positive, then the products in pair i are substitutes; otherwise, they are complements.<sup>14</sup> A higher value of ds<sub>i</sub> denotes a higher degree of substitution.

Each seller can sell her products separately or as a bundle (we show in the “Mixed Bundling” section that mixed bundling is dominated by either pure bundling or unbundling in our context). If a seller chooses to offer her products as a bundle, then she incurs a small bundling cost $\varepsilon > 0 .$ . The assumption regarding the bundling cost is a technical one. It enables us to resolve the equilibrium in favor of not bundling when a seller is indifferent between bundling and not bundling. Of greater importance, we make this assumption to eliminate any potential benefit from bundling such as the reduction in distribution and marketing cost cited in the prior literature. Clearly, any negative bundling cost (or benefit from bundling) will favor the bundling strategy.

The competition between the two sellers is modeled as a twostage game. In the first stage, both sellers simultaneously choose their bundling strategies. In the second stage, both sellers compete in prices. Our two-stage game reflects the fact that, for sellers, bundling decisions are usually more strategic and long-term compared to pricing decisions. This is because price decisions are more flexible in the sense that prices can be changed relatively easily, as compared to bundling decisions, which are more rigid. In our game, all parameters are common knowledge to both sellers.

Analogous to products in a competing pair, competing bundles also behave as complements or as substitutes. Bundles are complements if $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ , and are substitutes if $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

## Model Analysis

We solve for a subgame-perfect Nash equilibrium in pure strategies for the bundling game. Therefore, using backward induction, we solve for the equilibrium in the pricing subgame followed by the bundling equilibrium.

In the pricing subgame, we find that there is a unique equilibrium when the two competing products (or competing bundles) are substitutes. When the two products (bundles) are complements, multiple equilibria exist. In that case, we refine the equilibria using the Nash Bargaining Solution (NBS) concept (Nash 1950).<sup>15</sup> The equilibrium prices in the pricing subgame are as shown in Lemma 2 (p denotes the price of product $i ,$ and $p _ { b }$ denotes the price of a bundle).

## Lemma 2:

(a) If neither seller bundles her products, each will charge $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ for their product in pair i if the products in the pair are substitutes and if they are complements.v / 2

(b) If both sellers bundle, then each will charge $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 2 }$ for her bundle if the bundles behave as substitutes, and if they behave as complements.( )v v+ / 2 (c) If one seller bundles her products and the other does not, then the seller not bundling her products will charge $\bar { \nu _ { i } } - \nu _ { i }$ for her product in pair i if products in pair i are substitutes and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } / 2$ if they are complements, and the seller bundling her products will charge the sum of the two prices charged by the other seller.

The intuition underlying the above equilibrium prices is as follows: Consider the case where neither seller bundles her products. When the products in pair i are substitutes, the incremental value the consumer derives from buying the second product in the competing pair, $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ , is smaller than the value from the first product. The sellers do not have any incentive to set a price lower than $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ because the consumer will buy the product at this price even if the consumer has bought the other product. On the other hand, for any price higher than $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ , the seller that charges a higher price cannot make a sale for the following reason: Suppose seller 1 sets price $p _ { i 1 } \ge \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ and seller 2 decides to set price $p _ { i 1 } + \varepsilon$ $\varepsilon > 0$ The consumer gets a net utility of $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 }$ if she buys from only seller 1, a net utility of of $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } - \varepsilon$ if she buys only from seller 2, and a net utility of $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - 2 p _ { i 1 }$ if she buys from both sellers. Clearly, $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } > \nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } - \varepsilon$ , and when $p _ { i 1 } \ge \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } , \nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } > \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - 2 p _ { i 1 } - \varepsilon$ That is, the consumer realizes the highest net utility by buying only from the seller that has the lower price. The risk of losing the sale when the product is priced higher than $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ generates intense price competition between sellers, which reduces the price to $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ . When the products in a competing pair are complements, the incremental value from the second is more than the incremental value from the first. In this case, sellers do not have any incentive to engage in a price competition. Instead, they set prices to extract the entire consumer surplus. Consequently, any $( p _ { i 1 } , p _ { i 2 } )$ such that $p _ { i j } \ge \nu _ { i } ~ \forall j$ and $p _ { i 1 } + p _ { i 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$ is an equilibrium. When the products in a pair are complements, the pricing game is similar to the classic “splitting a $\mathrm { p i e } ^ { \prime \prime }$ game for which the NBS concept has been used widely to identify a plausible unique equilibrium (Rasmusen 2007, pp. 358-361). Applying the NBS refinement yields an equilibrium that divides the consumer surplus equally between the two sellers.

The equilibrium price when competing products are substitutes and when they are complements can be written as $( \nu _ { i } - d s _ { i } )$ and $( \nu _ { i } - d s _ { i } / 2 )$ , respectively. That is, for substitutes, for a given $\nu _ { i } ,$ a unit increase in the degree of substitution results in a unit decrease in price. In the case of complements, a unit increase in the degree of substitution results in only half a unit decrease in price, which implies a less intense price competition. We define the intensity of price competition as the net decrease in total price due to competition. Thus, if a product pair i consists of substitutes, it is equal to $d \boldsymbol { s } _ { i }$ , and if it consists of complements, it is $d s _ { i } / 2$ (alternatively denoted $d s _ { C } / 2 )$ . The combined intensity of price competition when products are sold separately is then equal to $d s _ { I } + d s _ { 2 }$ if both product pairs consist of substitutes and $( d s _ { I } + d s _ { 2 } ) / 2$ if both product pairs consist of complements. If one of the product pairs consists of substitutes and the other consists of complements, and the degree of substitution for the substitute and complement product pairs are denoted respectively as $d s _ { S }$ and $d s _ { C } ,$ then the combined intensity of price competition when products are sold separately is equal to $d s _ { S } \ – d s _ { C } / 2$

When both sellers bundle their products, the bundles compete as substitutes or as complements, depending on the value of buying a bundle from a single seller and buying bundles from both sellers. Thus, the price of a bundle in this case is obtained by simply substituting the value of bundles in the price expressions for the single product case. As for single products, the degree of substitution can be computed for bundles as well. The degree of substitution for a bundle, $d s _ { b } ,$ is given by $2 { \left( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \right) } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 }$ , which is equal to $d s _ { I } + d s _ { 2 }$ . Note that, using the degree of substitution for bundles, we can write the equilibrium price for substitute bundles and complement bundles as $\nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } - d s _ { b }$ and $\nu _ { \mathrm { 1 } } + \nu _ { \mathrm { 2 } } - d s _ { b } \ : / \ : 2$ , respectively. Thus, the intensity of price competition for the bundle is $d s _ { b }$ if the bundles are substitutes and $d s _ { b } / 2$ if the bundles are complements.

Interestingly, a seller does not enjoy the benefits of bundling if the other seller doesn’t bundle its products. Consider the case where one product pair consists of substitutes while the other consists of complements and the bundles behave as substitutes. A consequence of one (say, the first) seller not bundling its products is that bundled products from the other (second) seller compete with non-bundled products individually, as if they are not bundled. This is because if the sellers try to price the products as if they were being bundled by both of them (i.e., the first seller prices the products $\bar { \nu } _ { s } - \nu _ { s }$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { C } - \nu _ { C } .$ , respectively, and the second seller prices the bundle $\overline { { \nu } } _ { { s } } - \nu _ { { s } } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { { C } } - \nu _ { { C } } )$ , then the consumer will prefer to only purchase the substitute product from the first seller and nothing else; every other alternative to the consumer yields an inferior consumer surplus. In order to be able to sell their products, both sellers will have to reduce their prices (the first seller will reduce the price for the complement and the second seller will reduce the price for the bundle) until they arrive at the prices that prevail when neither seller bundles. This result is in sharp contrast to previous results which generally showed that a bundler obtains several competitive advantages over a nonbundler $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ Bakos and Brynjolfsson 2000; Nalebuff 2004). The different outcome is due to the homogeneous consumer setting considered here. In our setting, bundling does not reduce consumer diversity (which, in fact, cannot be reduced), which drove the earlier results.

The academic literature and popular press articles in the financial brokerage industry provide several pieces of evidence that support the main finding of Lemma 2(a). As discussed earlier, the execution services offered by brokerage houses are likely to be substitutes, and Lemma 2 predicts intense competition between these services along with low prices. After the deregulation of the brokerage commissions by the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) in 1975, Goldstein et al. (2009) found that the average execution commission rate for institutional investors fell from mid-teens (in cents per share) in the late 1970s to under 5 cents per share in 2004. Goldstein et al. further report the average number of low-cost brokers used by a client ranges from 8.8 for low volume clients to 13.3 for high volume clients. Thus, there is evidence that institutional clients spread execution across multiple brokerage firms. In the retail brokerage market, the average commission charged by an online broker fell from more than \$50 per trade in 1996 to less than \$15 per trade in 2000,<sup>16</sup> and commissions have decreased even further since. On the other hand, Goldstein et al. also report that brokerage houses that serve institutional investors use commissions not only to charge for execution but also for the research services they provide and that the average commission rates charged by brokerage houses that offer premium research services has not deceased as significantly as that charged by other brokerage houses.<sup>17</sup> A McKinsey & Company report states that in the general business-to-business information market, while firms that provide data or content alone enjoy a margin of only 10 percent, those that embed data in tools and solutions enjoy a margin of greater than 35 percent (Bensen-Armer and Hasker 2009).

Proposition 1 provides the equilibrium bundling strategies of sellers.

## Proposition 1:

(a) When both product pairs consist of substitutes, or both consist of complements, unbundling by both sellers is the unique Nash equilibrium.

(b) When one product pair consists of substitutes and the other consists of complements, bundling by both sellers and unbundling by both sellers are Nash equilibria, but bundling by both sellers Pareto-dominates unbundling by both.

Proposition 1 is the key result of this paper. It shows that when both products in a bundle have the same competitive structure (substitutes or complements) with their corresponding products, bundling by either seller is not an equilibrium. On the other hand, when products that have different competitive structures are bundled, bundling by both sellers emerges as the Pareto-dominant equilibrium. Interestingly, Proposition 1(a) holds regardless of whether the bundles themselves compete as substitutes or as complements. The intuition for this result can be explained in terms of the effect of bundling on the intensity of price competition. Recall that the combined intensity of price competition when both sellers sell the products separately is equal to $d s _ { I } + d s _ { 2 }$ if both product pairs consist of substitutes, $( d s _ { I } \ : + \ : d s _ { 2 } ) / 2$ if both consist of complements, and $d s _ { S } + d s _ { C } / 2$ if one consists of substitutes and the other consists of complements. Further, again as shown earlier, when both sellers sell their products as bundles, the intensity of price competition between bundles is $d s _ { I } ~ + ~ d s _ { 2 }$ if the bundles behave as substitutes, and $( d s _ { I } + d s _ { 2 } ) / 2$ if the bundles behave as complements. Observe that the intensity of overall price competition does not change when both products in the bundle consist of substitutes with their corresponding products or both consist of complements with their corresponding products. However, when the bundles have a substitute pair and a complement pair, the intensity of price competition reduces. In that case, the intensity of price competition for the bundles when they compete as substitutes, $d s _ { S } + d s _ { C } ,$ is less than the intensity of overall price competition when sellers sell their products separately, $d s _ { S } + d s _ { C } / 2 ,$ , since $d s _ { S } > 0$ and $d s _ { C } < 0$ Similarly, the intensity of price competition for the bundles when they compete as complements, $( d s _ { s } + d s _ { c } ) / 2$ , is again less than the intensity of overall price competition when sellers sell their products separately. Thus, bundling by both sellers softens price competition only when the bundles have a substitute pair and a complement pair. Since this benefits both sellers, bundling by both emerges as the unique Pareto-optimal equilibrium strategy. When bundling does not reduce the intensity of competition, such as when the bundles have two substitute pairs or two complement pairs, neither seller has any incentive to bundle; further, bundling costs the seller, and hence, both sellers sell their products separately.

Figure 1 graphically illustrates the intuition for our result. The figure shows, for any competing pair i, the equilibrium price as a function of the degree of substitution for that product. The equilibrium price for a product in a substitute pair with degree of substitution $d s _ { S } ( > 0 )$ is $p _ { s } ,$ and for a product in a complement pair with degree of substitution $d s _ { c } ( < 0 )$ it is $p _ { c } .$ As shown earlier, the price function has different slopes for products in substitute and complement pairs, and therefore it is concave piece-wise linear with an inflection at the y-axis (which is the boundary that separates complements and substitutes). When each seller offers a product that is a complement to the product in its competing pair and another product that is a substitute to the product in its competing pair separately, the average price per product is just the simple average of the product prices; this is shown as $p _ { u }$ in the figure. When these products are bundled, the bundles behave as substitutes if $d s _ { S } + d s _ { C } > 0$ , and as complements otherwise; in the figure, we assume $d s _ { S } + d s _ { C } > 0$ , but the same intuition holds when $d s _ { s } + d s _ { c } < 0 ( \mathrm { i } . \mathrm { e }$ , if the bundles behaved as complements). For the bundle, the average degree of substitution per product is $( d s _ { s } + d s _ { c } ) / 2$ , which results in the average price per product in the bundle to be $p _ { b } / 2$ as shown in the figure. The concavity of the price function ensures that $p _ { b } / 2 > p _ { u } .$ If both products in a bundle were substitutes or both were complements, then the firms would have operated in one linear part of the price function for both strategies, and there would be no difference in average prices. This illustrates why it is not sufficient for sellers to simply bundle a product from a less competitive market with a product from a more competitive market. Bundling of a substitute and a complement exploits the nonlinearity in the competitive structures of complements and substitutes and reduces the net intensity of competition. This reduction in the net intensity is essential for bundling to be profitable.

![](/api/attachments/CCNDYKWN/fulltext/images/6202ea29f1d35669de162666259a507c2140ba80ee23abb615fb8f29c0045e69.jpg)  
Figure 1. Price as a Function of Degree of Substitution (for a Substitute Pair and a Complement Pair)

Proposition 1 suggests that, in the financial brokerage industry, firms are likely to bundle research and execution. Empirical and anecdotal observations in this industry support this prediction. In the market that serves institutional investors, bundling of research and execution is the norm. As we stated earlier, the pricing model used by full-service brokerage houses is to charge clients a single fee through commissions for both research and trading services. Even in the discount brokerage market, firms, such as Charles Schwab which focus primarily on execution, have been providing access to extensive advisor networks in addition to their own research. However, such firms also practice a bundling strategy by requiring the advisor to be a customer and thus, as in the case of Schwab, “advisors have a financial incentive to encourage you to maintain your assets in custody with Schwab whereby Schwab receives additional compensation through trade commissions and fees.”<sup>18</sup> Industry experts in other information services industries also recommend firms to bundle raw data with tools and technologies that make sense of data to improve their profitability.<sup>19</sup>

The following result shows that, in a bundling equilibrium, consumers are worse off but the social welfare is unaffected compared to the scenario when at least one seller does not bundle a product that is a substitute with another that is a complement.

## Proposition 2:

When products in one pair are substitutes and those in the other are complements, compared to the scenario in which at least one seller does not bundle, when both sellers bundle,

(a) consumers are worse off, and

(b) the social welfare is unchanged.

It is clear from Proposition 2 that bundling a product that is a substitute to its competing product with one that is a complement to its competing product benefits the sellers at the expense of consumers. However, the social welfare is unaffected by the sellers’ strategy. The result is intuitive. In our model, whether the sellers bundle their products or unbundle, the consumers buy both products from both sellers in the equilibrium. Consequently, the utility realized by consumers from these products, which is also the social welfare, remains the same regardless of the seller strategy. The price paid by consumers simply represents the allocation of the social welfare to the two parties: sellers and consumer. A higher price indicates that sellers (consumers) capture a higher (smaller) portion of the social welfare. Since the price is higher in the bundling equilibrium compared to the unbundling scenario, consumers are worse off in the bundling equilibrium.

Propositions 1 and 2 reveal that, when competing sellers bundle a product that is a substitute to its competing product with a product that is a complement to its competing product, they benefit at the expense of consumers without altering the social welfare. This has significant implications for a policy maker. For instance, a current debate in the financial brokerage industry is whether the practice of bundling research and execution by brokerage houses should be regulated.<sup>20</sup> Our results show that a regulator of the financial brokerage industry cannot avoid taking the side of one of the parties— either the sellers or the consumers—if he chooses between allowing bundling and banning it.

In summary, we showed in this section that, in information markets, bundling of products that are substitutes (within their competing pair) and those that are complements (within their competing pair) by all competing sellers emerges as the Pareto-dominant equilibrium outcome. This outcome is the result of the distinctive characteristic of information markets, namely, consumers may find that even after buying a product from a seller, buying a competing product from another seller offers a positive value and in some instances the combined value of two products is greater than the sum of values of individual products. Bundling of complements and substitutes enables competing sellers to exploit this characteristic to soften price competition.

## Mixed Bundling

The analysis thus far has not considered the possibility of mixed bundling by sellers. In mixed bundling, a firm may offer the two products as a bundle as well as offer them individually.

Proposition 3: Either pure bundling or unbundling always weakly dominates mixed bundling.

The intuition for Proposition 3 is similar to that for Lemma 2(c): A seller does not enjoy the benefits of bundling if the other seller doesn’t offer only bundled products. A consequence of one (say, the first) seller offering mixed bundles and the other seller (the second) selling only bundled products is that bundled products from the second seller compete with non-bundled products individually of the first seller, as if they are not bundled. Consequently, both sellers set the prices that prevail when neither seller bundles. Thus, when both competing pairs consist of substitutes or complements, neither pure bundling nor mixed bundling is superior to unbundling. When one competing pair consists of substitutes and the other consists of complements, pure bundling by both is superior to both mixed bundling and unbundling.

An interesting implication of Proposition 3 is that banning sellers from offering only pure bundles will not benefit consumers. When sellers find it optimal to bundle their products, they will set the prices of individual products high enough such that in the equilibrium consumers will prefer to buy only the bundles.

## Model Extensions

We analyzed several extensions of our base model and found that our key result about the effect of bundling is robust to those variations. In the base model, we assumed the value to a consumer, if she buys a product from a single seller, is the same regardless of which seller she buys the product from. Further, the base analysis was restricted to two products for each seller. The primary reason for considering the twoproduct model is that the number of seller strategies increases exponentially with the number of products, and the model quickly becomes intractable. For instance, a seller offering three products has five strategies: sell each product separately, sell 1 and 2 as a bundle and 3 as a separate product, sell 1 and 3 as a bundle and 2 as a separate product, bundle 2 and 3 as a bundle and 1 as a separate product, and sell all three as one bundle. When both sellers are considered, a total of 25 combinations of strategies would have to be analyzed to derive the equilibrium. In the first extension, we analyze the case when consumer valuations across competing products are unequal and each seller sells more than two products. Further, in the base model, we assumed a homogenous consumer population with identical valuations for each product. In the second extension, we consider a model in which consumers are heterogeneous in their product valuations.

## Extension 1: Unequal Valuations Across Competing Products and More than Two Products from Each Seller

In this extension, we consider the case where each seller produces $n \geq 2$ products. Further, the consumer’s valuations of the two products in a competing pair are not necessarily identical. We impose the restriction that a seller will sell all her products as either one bundle of n products or as n separate products.<sup>21</sup> The timeline for the game remains the same as in the base model.

Let $\nu _ { i j }$ be the value to the consumer if she buys product i from seller j, and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$ be the value to the consumer if she buys product i from both sellers. We assume that $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } \geq \nu _ { i j } , \forall i , j$ . Let $p _ { i j }$ denote the price of product i from seller j, and $p _ { b j }$ denote the price of a bundle from seller j. Define $\bar { j } = 1 \mathrm { i f } j = 2$ and $\bar { j } = 2 \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } j = 1$ . Without loss of generality, let the products in each of the first t competing pairs consist of complements and those in the others consist of substitutes. The solution for the pricing sub game is as follows:

## Lemma 3:

(a) If neither seller bundles her products, then

$$
p _ {i j} = \overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i \bar {j}}, i f \overline {{v}} _ {i} \leq v _ {i 1} + v _ {i 2}
$$

(b) If both sellers bundle all their products, then

$$
p _ {b j} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i \bar {j}}\right) i f \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \bar {v} _ {i} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(v _ {i 1} + v _ {i 2}\right)
