$$

Lemma 3 shows that when consumer valuations of competing products are unequal, the product that has the higher valuation has a higher price compared to the other, regardless of whether the products in the pair are substitutes or complements.

The equilibrium in stage 1 of the game is as follows:

## Proposition 4:

(a) When all product pairs consist of substitutes, or all consist of complements, unbundling by both sellers is the unique Nash equilibrium.

(b) When at least one competing pair consists of substitutes and at least one consists of complements, bundling by both sellers and unbundling by both sellers are Nash equilibria, but bundling by both sellers Pareto-dominates unbundling by both.

Proposition 4 generalizes Proposition 1 for the case when each seller offers more than two products and the two products within a competing pair may have unequal consumer valuations. The key additional insight offered by Proposition 4 is that for bundling to benefit both sellers it is sufficient if bundles contain at least one pair that has substitute products and one pair that has complementary products.

We further note that the scenario in which one of the firms does not offer a competing product is a special case of extension 1. This scenario is mathematically equivalent to the firm offering a product with valuation equal to zero. For example, let firm 1offer a product $( \mathrm { e } . \mathrm { g } . , P )$ for which there is no competing product from firm 2. Every other product offered by firm 1 has a competing product from firm 2 and every product offered by firm 2 has a competing product from firm1. We can analyze this scenario by creating, for firm 2, a hypothetical product $( \mathrm { e } , \mathrm { g } . , P ^ { \prime } )$ , with a valuation equal to zero when it is consumed alone and an incremental valuation also equal to zero when it is consumed along with P. Proposition 4 applies to the model that includes the hypothetical product. Therefore, the insight that for bundling to benefit both sellers it is sufficient if bundles have at least one substitute pair and one complementary pair of products holds even when the firms offer different numbers of products, and some products do not have a competing product.

## Extension 2: Heterogeneous Consumer Valuations

In this extension, we consider a model in which consumers are heterogeneous in their product valuations. Specifically, let $\theta _ { i } \nu _ { i }$ be the value to the consumer if she buys product i from a single seller and $\theta _ { i } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$ be the value to the consumer if she buys product i from both sellers. Unlike the base model in which $\theta _ { i }$ was identical for all consumers, we assume in this extension that $\theta _ { i }$ are independent and uniformly distributed between 0 and 1. Furthermore, we assume that each seller has nproducts to offer. Again, we assume that the first t of these products compete as complements and others are substitutes.

In this setting, if products are sold individually, Sarvary and Parker (1997) showed that the profit to each seller from product i is $\frac { \big ( 3 \nu _ { i } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } \big ) \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) } { 4 \nu _ { i } }$ if $\overline { { \nu } } _ { _ i } < 2 \nu _ { _ i }$ and $\frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } } { 9 } \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } > \frac { 9 \nu _ { i } } { 4 }$ When $\nu _ { i } \leq \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } \leq \frac { 9 \nu _ { i } } { 4 }$ , no equilibria exist when consumers are heterogeneous.

Suppose both sellers bundle all their n products. The reservation price per good for buying a bundle from a single seller is given by the random variable $\frac { 1 } { n } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \theta _ { i } \nu _ { i }$ with mean ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \nu _ { i }$ The reservation price per good for buying bundles from both sellers has a mean $\frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$ . For sufficiently large $n ,$ the weak law of large numbers and the central limit theorem show that, for all consumers, the reservation price per good for a single bundle and both bundles converge to their means respectively with a probability approaching 1. Bakos and Brynjolfsson (1999) use a similar result to derive the impact of bundling information goods when consumers are heterogeneous. We assume that n is large enough to approximate $\frac { 1 } { n } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \theta _ { i } \nu _ { i }$ and $\frac { 1 } { n } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \theta _ { i } \bar { \nu } _ { i }$ by their means.

We will focus on situations where equilibria exist. We have the following proposition:

Proposition 5: When at least one product pair consists of substitutes and at least one product pair consists of complements, bundling by both sellers is the Pareto-dominant Nash equilibrium.

Proposition 5 demonstrates that the key result of the “Model Analysis” section regarding the impact of bundling on sellers holds even when consumer valuation is heterogeneous. The new insight derived from our analysis of Extension 2 is that the benefit of bundling a product that is a complement within its product pair with a product that is a substitute within its product pair can arise not only from a change in the competitive structure, as explained by our theory, but also from a reduction in consumer heterogeneity, a result explained by prior research in bundling. The added value of consumer heterogeneity in valuation, preserving the same mean valuation of $1 / 2 ,$ to the overall benefit of bundling can be determined as

(increase in profit because of bundling when $\theta _ { i }$ is uniformly distributed between 0 and 1) – (increase in profit because of bundling when $\theta _ { i } = 1 / 2$ for all i)

This added value can be computed as follows:

We consider the two cases: (1) when bundles are substitutes $( { \mathrm { i . e . } }$ , when $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } \leq 2 \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \nu _ { i } \mathbf { \epsilon } ) .$ , and (2) when bundles are complements (i.e., when $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } > 2 \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \nu _ { i } { \bf \epsilon } ) .$

(1) Bundles are substitutes: Profit when both sellers bundle and $\theta _ { i }$ is uniformly distributed between 0 and $1 \quad = { }$ $1 = { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ (5)

Profit when neither seller bundles and $\theta _ { i }$ is uniformly distributed between 0 and $1 = \frac { 1 } { 9 } \underset { i = 1 } { \overset { t } { \sum } } \bar { \nu } _ { i } + \underset { i = t + 1 } { \overset { n } { \sum } } \frac { \big ( 3 \nu _ { i } + \bar { \nu } _ { i } \big ) \big ( \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) } { 4 \nu _ { i } }$ (6)

Profit when both sellers bundle and $\theta _ { i } = 1 / 2$ for all $i \ =$ ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ (7)

Profit when neither seller bundles and $\theta _ { i } = 1 / 2$ for all $i =$ ${ \frac { 1 } { 4 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } { \bar { \nu } } _ { i } + { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \sum _ { i = t + 1 } ^ { n } \bigl ( { \bar { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr )$ (8)

Therefore, the added value of consumer heterogeneity in valuation to the overall benefit of bundling is given by $[ ( 5 ) -$ (6)] – [(7) – (8)], which simplifies to $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } \frac { 5 \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } } { 3 6 } + \sum _ { j = t + 1 } ^ { n } \frac { \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) ^ { 2 } } { 4 \nu _ { i } }$

(b) Bundles are complements: If neither seller bundles, the profit expressions when $\theta _ { i }$ is uniformly distributed between 0 and 1 and when $\theta _ { i } = 1 / 2$ are identical to the corresponding ones given for the case when bundles are substitutes (that is, those in (6) and (8)). If both sellers bundle, the seller profit is equal to ${ \frac { 1 } { 4 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i }$ whether $\cdot \theta _ { i }$ is uniformly distributed between 0 and 1 or $\theta _ { i } = 1 / 2$ . Therefore, the added value of consumer heterogeneity in valuation to the overall benefit of bundling simplifies to the expression for the case when bundles are substitutes.

It is straightforward to see that consumer heterogeneity in valuation enhances the overall benefit of bundling in the information products market.

## Other Extensions

We have also analyzed other extensions to the model. For instance, our main results are not qualitatively affected even when products within a bundle behave as substitutes or complements. This is because the interaction effects of products within a bundle do not impact the competitive structure across bundles. Although we normalized the fixed and marginal production costs to zero, our results hold for any positive symmetric cost as long as it is profitable for each seller to sell every product separately. The assumption about a small positive bundling cost guarantees that when sellers are indifferent between bundling and unbundling strategies, unbundling dominates bundling. For example, if the cost of bundling is zero, then when one seller unbundles her products, the other seller will be indifferent between bundling and unbundling. A positive bundling cost resolves the tie in the favor of unbundling in this case. Clearly, a negative bundling cost (i.e., benefit from bundling) favors bundling.

## Managerial Implications

We have shown how competing sellers can use the bundling strategy to mitigate price competition in information markets. We showed that bundling of products that compete as complements with those that compete as substitutes reduces the net intensity of price competition, and bundling becomes the (Pareto-dominant) equilibrium strategy.

Our theory is motivated by the observation that consumers often buy more than one competing product in information markets and, furthermore, several sellers offer competing product bundles. In particular, we found that the financial brokerage services industry offers several examples that fit our theory well and therefore we used it to illustrate our theoretical model and results. In addition to providing a potential explanation for the prevalence of the bundling strategy in this industry, our research offers several implications for policy makers and sellers in this industry. For instance, there is a movement in the financial services industry to unbundle the research services from execution. For instance, in November 2013, the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) of the United Kingdom outlined its plan to conduct a rigorous review of the use of client commissions to pay for research and proposed preliminary changes.<sup>22</sup> The FCA explicitly recognized “lack of effective competition for services bundled with execution, such as third-party research,” in addition to others such as lack of transparency and accountability, as a motivation for the proposed changes. Our paper articulates a potential mechanism by which bundling of research and execution affects competition. Our theory predicts that if the FCA is successful in completely unbundling these two services in this market, the competition for execution will intensify and the trading commission will decrease further. In addition, the pricing of research services will reflect the value they offer to clients more accurately.<sup>23</sup> Our theory also predicts that consumers will benefit from the unbundling of execution and research.

Our results have implications for sellers in information markets deciding their product design strategies. Clearly, sellers would benefit by bundling information products that compete as complements with those that compete as substitutes. As we showed in this paper, the precision of information and the correlation between information from competing sellers play a key role in determining the nature of competition, and therefore sellers’ choices regarding the precision and correlation dimensions of information will affect the profitability from the bundling strategy. For information to compete as complements, both precision and correlation cannot be too high. This suggests that sellers in information markets may not always have an incentive to create information products that have a high degree of precision, and competing sellers may create information using contrarian assumptions and analysis methods to reduce correlation. However, a low precision reduces the intrinsic value of information. Therefore, sellers will need to carefully analyze the tradeoff between the intrinsic value of an information product and the competitive landscape while designing their products.

Our results suggest sellers in information markets can increase profits by judiciously identifying products to bundle. Suppose two sellers sell the same types of information which are highly correlated. These sellers may find it profitable to develop analytics tools that are tightly linked to the data they sell, and bundle the tools with the data. Analogously, sellers of analytics tools with similar capabilities can bundle these tools to different information types. For example, sellers of demand forecast information for a product may link their forecast data to customized forecasting tools that exploit the characteristics of data they sell; on the other hand, sellers of similar general purpose forecasting tools can link their tools to different specialized information types that are used in the forecasting model—say, one may focus on selling consumer demographic data and another may focus on selling expert opinions, both of which may help with forecasting demand.

We find that sellers in information markets enjoy the enhanced benefit from bundling only when there are competing sellers and both bundle. This suggests that information markets may be characterized by a large number of firms that adopt a uniform, rather than differentiated, product design strategy. Even a new entrant in this market that may initially focus on a single product is likely to find that adding other products to its product line and offering product bundles will boost the profitability of the entire product line. For example, Charles Schwab started as a discount trader offering only execution service and later added research to its portfolio of products. Schwab is now a full-fledged broker that bundles execution and research services.

Finally, in order to illustrate the positive impact of bundling information products on competing sellers, we assume a base model that is unfavorable to bundling. For instance, we assume that there is a cost associated with bundling whereas in the prior literature, benefits related to economies of scale, and savings in advertising, packaging, and distribution costs, have been cited as potential reasons for firms to bundle. We also assume that consumers have identical reservation prices so that the homogenization of consumer valuations is absent from our base model. In most of the prior literature, such homogenization of consumer valuations was one of the main driving forces behind the benefit from bundling. Even when these benefits of bundling are absent, we show that bundling by both sellers emerges as an equilibrium strategy when substitutes and complements are bundled. When the above benefits of bundling are present in information markets, (for instance, we show in Extension 2 in the “Model Extensions” section that when consumers are heterogeneous, sellers realize a higher benefit from bundling compared to when consumers are homogeneous), the attractiveness of bundling increases. This suggests that when other benefits of bundling are present, bundling may emerge as the equilibrium strategy even when only substitutes or only complements are bundled.

## Conclusions and Directions for Future Research

We showed how competing sellers can use a bundling strategy to mitigate price competition in information markets. Although we illustrated our model and discussed the results in the context of the financial brokerage industry, our model and results may apply to several other markets. Our results are particularly applicable to information products because information from different competing sources could either substitute or complement each other, depending on their accuracies and the correlations between them. For instance, the three major credit bureaus, Equifax, Experian, and Transunion, sell bundles that include credit reports and proprietary credit scores. When evaluating a loan or credit card application, financial institutions often get the bundled credit information from multiple credit bureaus. Bundling of credit history and proprietary credit score is common in this industry. Similarly, market research firms such as AC Nielsen and IRI bundle decision support models and software with raw market research data. In all these examples, consumers often buy bundles from more than one seller. While we cannot conclude that mitigation of competition is the only reason for these sellers to bundle their products, our analysis posits one potential explanation for bundling that deserves to be empirically investigated. Furthermore, we believe that our findings are also applicable in other contexts where decision making involves data and interpretation of data and there is a market for data and for interpretation services, such as business consulting, healthcare, and legal services.

To our knowledge this is the first paper that analyzes the economic impact of product design strategy (specifically, product bundling) in information markets, and it raises several interesting directions for future research. For instance, the paper assumes that the sellers have created products with specific precision and correlation levels, and it only analyzes the impact of bundling them. The question of how to choose (endogenize) the quality or correlation is a significant one that warrants further research. Along similar lines, whether sellers should offer more aggregate summary level information or more detailed raw data, and more generically how to slice and dice information to be sold as individual units, deserves additional research. The increasing significance of the information product markets and the unique nature of competition in these markets suggest that product design issues such as the ones mentioned above will likely become critical to firms that are engaged in selling information. Another interesting extension to our research lies in exploring the role of intermediaries in the bundling of information products. Consider, for example, Charles Schwab, which offers bundles of information from providers such as Quote.Com and MarketEdge. These information providers sell some of their products independently and some through intermediaries such as Schwab. It is worth investigating the optimal channel strategy for these information providers and intermediaries.

On the modeling side, our research has a few limitations as summarized in Table 2. We have used a stylized duopoly model to analyze the effect of bundling. The model, in addition to being tractable, provides several interesting and significant insights. In a duopoly, the value to a consumer can be viewed as concave (convex) in the number of competing substitute (complement) products bought. If the duopoly is relaxed to more than two sellers, the consumers’ valuation function may not be either a strictly concave or strictly convex function throughout the number of competing products. So long as the consumer valuation function is either concave or convex throughout, the qualitative results of our analysis will hold. Next, we assumed that the information provided by sellers is unbiased: the expected value of the information provided is identical to the true value. If the information is biased, the best combined estimator may not be the simple mean of the two estimates as given by Equation (1). If the bias is known, the consumer can de-bias the information using an appropriate scaling factor before applying Equation (1). Future research should examine strategies to mitigate the unknown bias before using the information for decision making. We used the (inverse of) variance of information as the measure of information quality. While this measure is reasonable in an estimation context, other quality measures can be used to generalize our results. We used a simultaneous game. It is quite possible that some firms may move first and other firms may follow while choosing the bundling strategy and setting prices. An interesting extension would be to explore the sequential game to understand the first-mover and second-mover advantages and disadvantages. We can extend the model to a three-tier structure with an information aggregator that serves as an intermediary between content providers and consumers. Finally, endogenizing the quality and correlation characteristics of information would add richer insights about product design strategies in the information product markets.

This will affect the shape of the consumer utility as a function of the number of products bought, and thus the degree of substitution and complementarity.</td><td>The model can be extended to a market with more than two sellers. ## About the Authors

Srinivasan Raghunathan is a professor of Information Systems in the Naveen Jindal School of Management, The University of Texas at Dallas. His current research interests are in the economics of information security and economic impact of e-commerce technologies such as review platforms and recommendation systems. His papers have been published in a variety of journals including Management Science, Operations Research, Information Systems Research, Decision Analysis, Journal of MIS, Decision Support Systems, various IEEE transactions, IIE transactions, European Journal of Operational Research, and Production and Operations Management. He has served on the editorial boards of Information Systems Research, Information Technology and Management, and Journal of Electronic Commerce Research.

Sumit Sarkar is the Charles and Nancy Davidson Chair and professor of Information Systems in the Naveen Jindal School of Management at the University of Texas at Dallas. He received his Ph.D. from the Simon School of Business at the University of Rochester, his MBA from the Indian Institute of Management Calcutta, and B. Tech from the Indian Institute of Technology Delhi. His research interests are in information quality, business intelligence, personalization and recommendation technologies, sponsored search, data privacy, heterogeneous databases, and software release strategies. His research has appeared in MIS Quarterly, Management Science, Information Systems Research, ACM Transactions on Database Systems, Operations Research, IEEE Transactions on Knowledge and Data Engineering, The INFORMS Journal on Computing, and several other journals and conference proceedings. He has been a visiting faculty member at the Indian School of Business, and a visiting scientist at IBM Research Laboratories. Sumit is a Distinguished Fellow of the Information Systems Society of INFORMS.

# COMPETITIVE BUNDLING IN INFORMATION MARKETS: A SELLER-SIDE ANALYSIS

Srinivasan Raghunathan and Sumit Sarkar Naveen Jindal School of Management, The University of Texas at Dallas, Richardson, TX 75080 U.S.A. {sraghu@utdallas.edu} {sumit@utdallas.edu}

## Appendix A

## Proofs for Lemmas and Propositions

Proof for Lemma 1

Substitution of Equation (2) in Equation (3) and algebraic rearrangement gives the result.

## Proof for Lemma 2

## Case A: Neither Seller Bundles Products

We derive prices by first deriving the seller reaction functions, $R _ { i 1 } ( p _ { i 2 } )$ and $R _ { i 2 } ( p _ { i 1 } )$ , for sellers 1 and 2, respectively, when the products in the $i ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ competing pair are substitutes and when they are complements. The intersection of these reaction curves represents the Nash equilibrium.

## (i) Products are substitutes (i.e., $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } \leq 2 \nu _ { i } )$ ).

We consider only the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { i j } ,$ which is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } , \nu _ { i } \right)$

If one seller charges more than $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ , then the other seller’s optimal price is to charge slightly less than the first seller’s price for the following reason: Suppose the first seller charges $p _ { i 1 } > \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ . If the second seller charges more than $p _ { i 1 } \left( \mathbf { e . g . , } p _ { i 1 } + \delta \right)$ , the consumer’s surplus or net utility from buying the product (i) only from firm 1, (ii) only from firm 2, and (iii) from both firms, are as follows:

(a) $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 }$ when the consumer buys from only seller 1

(b) $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } - \delta$ when the consumer buys from only seller 2

(c) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - 2 p _ { i 1 } - \delta < 0$ when the consumer buys from both seller 1 and seller 2

Clearly, the consumer realizes maximum surplus by buying from only seller 1 and hence profit to seller 2 is zero.

If the second seller charges $p _ { i 1 } .$ , the consumer’s surplus or net utility from buying the product (a) only from firm 1, (b) only from firm 2, and (c) from both firms, are as follows:

(a) $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 }$ when the consumer buys from only seller 1

(b) $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } + \delta$ when the consumer buys from only seller 2

(c) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - 2 p _ { i 1 } + \delta < 0$ when the consumer buys from both seller 1 and seller 2

Clearly, the consumer is indifferent between buying from only seller 1 and buying only from seller 2. Assuming that the consumer chooses the two sellers with equal probability, profit to seller 2 is $p _ { i 1 } / 2$

If the second seller charges less than $p _ { i 1 } \left( \mathbf { e . g . , } p _ { i 1 } - \delta \right)$ , the consumer’s surplus or net utility from buying the product (a) only from firm 1, (b) only from firm 2, and (c) from both firms, are as follows:

(a) $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 }$ when the consumer buys from only seller 1

(b) $\nu _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } + \delta$ when the consumer buys from only seller 2

(c) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - 2 p _ { i 1 } + \delta < 0$ when the consumer buys from both seller 1 and seller 2

Clearly, the consumer realizes maximum surplus by buying from only seller 2 and hence profit to seller 2 is $p _ { i 1 } - \delta$

For any $p _ { i 1 } .$ , there always exists a $\delta < { p _ { i 1 } } / { 2 }$ such that setting a price $p _ { i 1 } - \delta$ is optimal for seller 2.

Suppose one seller charges $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$ , then the other seller will charge $\bar { \nu _ { i } } - \nu _ { i }$ for the same reason explained above. Thus, we obtain the following reaction functions:

$$
R _ {i 1} \left(p _ {i 2}\right) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}, i f p _ {i 2} = \bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i} \\ p _ {2 i} - \delta , i f p _ {i 2} > \bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i} \end{array} \right. \quad R _ {i 2} \left(p _ {i 1}\right) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}, i f p _ {i 1} = \bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i} \\ p _ {1 i} - \delta , i f p _ {i 1} > \bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i} \end{array} \right.
$$

The reaction curves intersect at $p _ { i 1 } = p _ { i 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$

## (ii) Products are complements $( i . e . , \ \bar { \nu } _ { i } > \nu _ { i } )$

The feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { i 1 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { i } , \bar { \nu _ { i } } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ . In this region, $R _ { i j } \big ( p _ { i \bar { j } } \big ) = \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - p _ { i \bar { j } } , \forall j$ , where $\bar { j } = 2 \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } j = 1$ . The reaction curves intersect between the points $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } , \nu _ { i } \right)$ and $\left( \nu _ { i } , \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ . The equation of the intersection line is given by $p _ { i 1 } + p _ { i 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$ , and the set of points in this line constitute the Nash equilibria.

In the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { i 1 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { i 1 } - \nu _ { i } \big ) \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } - \nu _ { i } \big )$ and $p _ { i 2 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { i 2 } - \nu _ { i } \big ) \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - p _ { i 2 } - \nu _ { i } \big )$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the unique equilibrium stated in the proposition.

## Case B: Both Sellers Bundle Products

The proof for equilibrium bundle prices is similar to that for Case A after substituting the values for bundles in place of values for the $i ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ product.

## Case C: One Seller Does Not Bundle and the Other Seller Bundles

Assume seller 1 bundles and seller 2 does not bundle. We consider three subcases: (i) products in both competing pairs are substitutes, (ii) products in both competing pairs are complements, and (iii) products in one pair are substitutes and the products in the other pair are complements.

## (i) Products in both competing pairs are substitutes:

For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { i 2 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } , \nu _ { i } \right)$ . For seller 1, the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { i 1 }$ is given by $\left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } ) , \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \nu _ { i } \right)$ . The following reaction functions can be derived by using reasoning similar to that for Case A.

$$
\begin{array}{l} R _ {b 1} \left(p _ {1 2}, p _ {2 2}\right) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right), i f p _ {i 2} = \bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}, \forall i \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} p _ {i 2} - \delta , i f \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} p _ {i 2} > \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \end{array} \right. \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} R _ {i 2} \left(p _ {b 1}\right) = \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) i f p _ {b 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right), \forall i \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} R _ {i 2} \left(p _ {b 1}\right) = p _ {b 1} - \delta i f p _ {b 1} > \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \end{array}
$$

Note that in the last equation of the above reaction functions, when seller 1 charges a price higher than $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ , seller 2 can sell b oth its products by setting a total price which is slightly below the bundle price set by seller 1.

The reaction curves intersect at $p _ { b 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , p _ { i 2 } = \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big )$

(ii) Products in both competing pairs are complements: The feasible and non-dominated regions are given by $\left( \nu _ { i } , \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) \right)$ and $\left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \nu _ { i } , \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) \right)$ for $p _ { i 2 }$ and $p _ { b 1 } ,$ respectively. In this region, $R _ { b 1 } \big ( p _ { 1 2 } , p _ { 2 2 } \big ) = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } p _ { i 2 } \mathrm { a n d } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } R _ { i 2 } \big ( p _ { b 1 } \big ) = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) - p _ { b 1 }$ An analysis similar to that for Case A gives the unique symmetric equilibrium $p _ { b 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } p _ { i 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } } { 2 }$

(iii) Products in one pair are substitutes and products in the other pair are complements.

We assume, without loss of generality, that products in pair 1 are substitutes and the products in pair 2 are complements. The feasible and nondominated regions for $p _ { 1 2 }$ and $p _ { 2 2 } ,$ respectively, are given by $\left( \left( \hat { \nu } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) , \nu _ { i } \right)$ and $\left( \nu _ { 2 } , \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right) \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { b 1 }$ is given by $\left( \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) + \nu _ { 2 } , \nu _ { 1 } + \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right) \right)$ . In equilibrium, seller 1 will set a price for the bundle such that the bundle is sold. Consequently, the maximum value the buyer derives from product 1 from seller $2 \mathrm { i s } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ . Thus, $p _ { 1 2 } = \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ , and the consumer will buy product 1 from seller 2 regardless of the price of the bundle. Consequently, given p<sub>22</sub>, $p _ { b 1 } = \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } = p _ { 2 2 }$ , and, given p<sub>b1</sub>, $p _ { 2 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } - p _ { b 1 }$ The unique equilibrium under NBS on this line is given by $p _ { b 1 } - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) = p _ { 2 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$

## Proof for Proposition 1

We consider the following three cases: (i) when products in each competing pair are substitutes, (ii) when products in each pair are complements, and (iii) when products in the first pair are substitutes and the products in the second pair are complements. In each of these cases, we show the product policy game in strategic form and then derive the equilibrium strategy.

(i) When products in each pair are substitutes, the payoff table is given by the following:

<table><tr><td></td><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td>Bundle</td></tr><tr><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{2} \overline{v_i} - v_i, \sum_{i=1}^{2} \overline{v_i} - v_i \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{2} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_i \right), \sum_{i=1}^{2} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_i \right) - \varepsilon \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>Bundle</td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{2} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_i \right) - \varepsilon, \sum_{i=1}^{2} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_i \right) \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{2} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_i \right) - \varepsilon, \sum_{i=1}^{2} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_i \right) - \varepsilon \right)$ </td></tr></table>

It is easy to see from the payoff table that the Nash equilibrium is (do-not-bundle, do-not-bundle).

(ii) When products in each pair are complements, the payoff table is as shown below.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td>Bundle</td></tr><tr><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{2} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2}, \sum_{i=1}^{2} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{2} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2}, \sum_{i=1}^{2} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} - \varepsilon \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>Bundle</td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{2} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} - \varepsilon, \sum_{i=1}^{2} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{2} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} - \varepsilon, \sum_{i=1}^{2} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} - \varepsilon \right)$ </td></tr></table>

It clearly follows that the Nash equilibrium is (do-not-bundle, do-not-bundle).

(iii) When products in the first pair are substitutes, and the products in the second pair are complements, we obtain the following payoff table.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td>Bundle</td></tr><tr><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td> $\left( \overline{v_1} - v_1 + \frac{\overline{v_2}}{2}, \overline{v_1} - v_1 + \frac{\overline{v_2}}{2} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \overline{v_1} - v_1 + \frac{\overline{v_2}}{2}, \overline{v_1} - v_1 + \frac{\overline{v_2}}{2} - \varepsilon \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>Bundle</td><td> $\left( \overline{v_1} - v_1 + \frac{\overline{v_2}}{2} - \varepsilon, \overline{v_1} - v_1 + \frac{\overline{v_2}}{2} \right)$ </td><td> $\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \left( \overline{v_1} - v_1 + \overline{v_2} - v_2 - \varepsilon, \overline{v_1} - v_1 + \overline{v_2} - v_2 - \varepsilon \right) \text{ if bundles are substitutes} \\ \left( \frac{\overline{v_1} + \overline{v_2}}{2} - \varepsilon, \frac{\overline{v_1} + \overline{v_2}}{2} - \varepsilon \right) \text{ if bundles are complements} \end{array} \right.$ </td></tr></table>

Because $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } > \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } < \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$ (bundle, bundle) and (do-not-bundle, do-not-bundle) are both Nash equilibria regardless of whether the bundles behave as complements or as substitutes. Comparing the profits under these two equilibria shows that (bundle, bundle) is Pareto dominant.

## Proof for Proposition 2

Assume product 1 is a substitute and product 2 is a complement. In the bundling equilibrium, the consumer buys bundles from both sellers. Therefore, using the bundle price given in Lemma 2, we compute the consumer surplus and social welfare as follows:

${ \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { 2 { \big ( } \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } { \big ) } - { \overline { { \nu _ { 1 } } } } - { \overline { { \nu _ { 2 } } } } } \\ { 0 { \mathrm { ~ i f ~ t h e ~ b u n d l e s ~ a r ~ } } } \end{array} \right. }$ if the bundles are substitutes Consumer surplus in the bundling equilibrium = e complements

Social welfare in the bundling equilibrium = $\overline { { \nu _ { 1 } } } + \overline { { \nu _ { 2 } } }$

In the unbundling scenario, the consumer still buys both products from each seller. Using the unbundled prices given in Lemma 2, we compute the consumer surplus and social welfare as the following:

Consumer surplus in the unbundling equilibrium = $2 \nu _ { 1 } - \overline { { \nu _ { 1 } } }$

Social welfare in the unbundling equilibrium $= \overline { { \nu _ { _ 1 } } } + \overline { { \nu _ { _ 2 } } }$

Clearly, the consumer surplus is smaller under the bundling equilibrium than under unbundling because $2 \nu _ { 2 } - \overline { { \nu _ { 2 } } } < 0$ . Furthermore, there is no difference in the social welfare in the two scenarios.

## Proof for Proposition 3

Without loss of generality, we show that either pure bundling or unbundling weakly dominates mixed bundling for seller 1, regardless of seller 2’s strategy.

Suppose seller 1 uses mixed bundling. We make the following observations.

(a) If the consumer buys the bundle from seller 1, then she will not buy either product 1 or product 2 individually from seller 1 at any positive price because the incremental value from either of these two individual products is zero.

(b) If the consumer does not buy the bundle from seller 1, then she will buy both products individually from seller 1 in the equilibrium because if a product is not bought then seller 1 can strictly increase its profit by setting a small price of ε > 0 and inducing the consumer to buy.

Now consider case (a). The equilibrium prices in the pricing subgame case are identical to

(i) those given in Lemma 2(b) if seller 2 sells only the pure bundle – both sellers obtain the same equilibrium profit as that when both sellers are pure bundlers.

(ii) those given in Lemma 2(c) if seller 2 sells only individual products—seller 2 obtains the same equilibrium profit as that when both sellers are pure unbundlers but seller 1 obtains less than the equilibrium profit of pure unbundlers because of the bundling cost.

(iii) those given in Lemma 2(b) if seller 2 also uses the mixed bundling strategy and it is profitable for seller 2 to sell the bundle than the individual products (i.e., when one product pair is a substitute and the other pair is a complement)—both sellers obtain the same equilibrium profit as that when both sellers are pure bundlers.

(iv) those given on Lemma 2(c) if seller 2 also uses the mixed bundling strategy and it is profitable for seller 2 to sell the individual product than the bundle (i.e., when both product pairs are either substitutes or complements)—both sellers obtain less than the equilibrium profit of pure unbundlers because of the bundling cost.

Clearly, in case (a), regardless of seller 2’s strategy (i) – (iv), seller 1’s prices and profit are never higher than to those when it uses pure bundling or pure unbundling.

Now consider case (b). The equilibrium prices in the pricing sub game case are identical to

(i) those given in Lemma 2(c) if seller 2 sells only the pure bundle—both sellers obtain less than the equilibrium profit of pure unbundlers because of the bundling cost.

(ii) those given in Lemma 2(a) if seller 2 sells only individual products—seller 2 obtains the same equilibrium profit as that when both sellers are pure unbundlers but seller 1 obtains less than the equilibrium profit of pure unbundlers because of the bundling cost.

(iii) those given in Lemma 2(c) if seller 2 also uses the mixed bundling strategy and it is profitable for seller 2 to sell the bundle rather than the individual products (i.e., when one product pair is a substitute and the other pair is a complement)—both sellers obtain less than the equilibrium profit of pure unbundlers because of the bundling cost.

(iv) those given on Lemma 2(a) if seller 2 also uses the mixed bundling strategy and the bundle price is not higher than sum of the prices of individual products for seller 2 (i.e., when both product pairs are either substitutes or complements)—both sellers obtain less than the equlibrium profit of pure unbundlers because of the bundling cost.

Clearly, in case (b), as in case (a), regardless of seller 2’s strategy (i) – (iv), seller 1’s prices and profit are never higher than those when it uses pure bundling or pure unbundling.

## Proof for Lemma 3

Assume without loss of generality $\nu _ { i 1 } > \nu _ { i 2 }$ . We prove Lemma 3 by deriving the seller reaction functions, $R _ { i l } ( p _ { i 2 } )$ and $R _ { i 2 } ( p _ { i I } )$ , for sellers 1 and 2, respectively, under various conditions. The intersection of these reaction curves represents the Nash equilibrium

Case (a)

$$
\text {(i)} \quad \bar {v} _ {i} \leq v _ {i 1} + v _ {i 2}
$$

$$
R _ {i 1} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 2} \text {if} p _ {i 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 1} \\ v _ {i 1} - v _ {i 2} + p _ {i 2} - \varepsilon , \varepsilon > 0, \text {if} \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 1} <   p _ {i 2} \leq v _ {i 2} \\ v _ {i 1} \text {if} p _ {i 2} > v _ {i 2} \end{array} \right.
$$

$$
R _ {i 2} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 1} \text {if} p _ {i 1} <   \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 2} \\ v _ {i 2} - v _ {i 1} + p _ {i 1} - \varepsilon , \varepsilon > 0, \text {if} \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 1} <   p _ {i 1} <   v _ {i 1} \\ v _ {i 2} \text {if} p _ {i 1} > v _ {i 1} \end{array} \right.
$$

Hence, the Nash equilibrium prices are $\overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \nu _ { i 2 }$ and $\overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \nu _ { i 1 }$ for sellers 1 and 2 respectively.

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } > \nu _ { i 1 } + \nu _ { i 2 }$

$$
R _ {i 1} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 2} \text {if} p _ {i 2} \leq v _ {i 2} \\ \overline {{v _ {i}}} - p _ {i 2} \text {if} v _ {i 2} <   p _ {i 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 1} \\ v _ {i 1} \text {if} p _ {i 2} > \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 1} \end{array} \right.
$$

$$
R _ {i 2} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 1} \text {if} p _ {i 1} \leq v _ {i 1} \\ \overline {{v _ {i}}} - p _ {i 1} \text {if} v _ {i 1} <   p _ {i 1} \leq \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 2} \\ v _ {i 2} \text {if} p _ {i 1} > \overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i 2} \end{array} \right.
$$

The reaction curves intersect between the points $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i 2 } , { \nu } _ { i 2 } \right)$ and $\left( \nu _ { i 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i 1 } \right)$ . The equation of the intersection line is given by $p _ { i 1 } + p _ { i 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$ , and the set of points in this line constitute the Nash equilibria. In the NBS solution, $p _ { i 1 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { i 1 } - \nu _ { i 1 } \big ) \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - p _ { i 1 } - \nu _ { i 2 } \big )$ and $p _ { i 2 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { i 2 } - \nu _ { i 2 } \big ) \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - p _ { i 2 } - \nu _ { i 1 } \big )$ . Solving the above optimization problem gives the unique equilibrium stated in the Lemma.

## Case (b)

The proof for Lemma 3 (b) is similar to that of Lemma 2 (b) after substitution of the reservation price values for bundles in place of reservation price values for the $i ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ product.

Case (c)

The feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { i 2 }$ is given by $\left( { \left( \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - { \nu _ { i 1 } } \right) , { \nu _ { i 2 } } } \right) , { \forall i \in \left\{ { t + 1 , . . . , n } \right\} }$ and $\Big ( \nu _ { i 2 } , \Big ( \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \nu _ { i 1 } \Big ) \Big ) , \forall i \in \{ 1 , . . . , t \}$ . The feasible and nondominated region for $p _ { b 1 }$ is given by $\left( \sum _ { i = t + 1 } ^ { n } \left( \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \nu _ { i 1 } \right) + \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } \nu _ { i 1 } , \sum _ { i = t + 1 } ^ { n } \nu _ { i 1 } + \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } \left( \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \nu _ { i 1 } \right) \right)$

In equilibrium, the consumer will buy the bundle and all products from the non-bundler. Otherwise, the sellers can increase their profit by setting the price of the product not bought to the incremental value it provides in which case the consumer will buy it.

Therefore, $p _ { i 2 } = \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \nu _ { i 1 } \forall i \in \{ t + 1 , . . . , n \}$ , and the consumer will buy products $t + 1$ through n from seller 2 irrespective of the price of the bundle. Consequently, given $p _ { i 2 } , i \in \left\{ t + 1 , . . . , n \right\} , p _ { b 1 } = \sum _ { i = t + 1 } ^ { n } \left( \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \nu _ { i 2 } \right) + \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } p _ { i 2 }$ , and given p<sub>b1</sub>, $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } p _ { i 2 } = \sum _ { i = t + 1 } ^ { n } \left( \overline { { { \nu _ { i } } } } - \nu _ { i 2 } \right) + \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } \overline { { { \nu _ { i } } } } - p _ { b 1 }$ The equilibrium under NBS is given by $p _ { b 1 } = \sum _ { i = t + 1 } ^ { n } \left( \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } - \nu _ { i 2 } \right) + \frac { \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } } { 2 } \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } p _ { i 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { t } \left( \frac { \overline { { \nu _ { i } } } } { 2 } + \frac { \nu _ { i 2 } - \nu _ { i 1 } } { 2 } \right)$

## Proof for Proposition 4

We consider the following three cases: (i) when products in all competing pairs are substitutes, (ii) when products in all pairs are complements, and (iii) when products in the first t pairs are complements and the products in the rest of the pairs are substitutes. In each of these cases, we show the product policy game in strategic form and then derive the equilibrium strategy.

(i) When products in all pairs are substitutes, the payoff table is given by the following:

<table><tr><td></td><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td>Bundle</td></tr><tr><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right), \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i1} \right) \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right), \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i1} \right) - \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>Bundle</td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right) - \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}, \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i1} \right) \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right) - \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}, \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i1} \right) - \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} \right)$ </td></tr></table>

It is easy to see from the payoff table that the Nash equilibrium is (do-not-bundle, do-not-bundle).

(ii) When products in all pairs are complements, the payoff table is as shown below.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td>Bundle</td></tr><tr><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \right), \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i2} - v_{i1}}{2} \right) \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \right), \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i2} - v_{i1}}{2} \right) - \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>Bundle</td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \right) - \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}, \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i2} - v_{i1}}{2} \right) \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \ right) - \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}, \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i2} - v_{i1}}{2} \right) - \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} \right)$ </td></tr></table>

As for case (i), it clearly follows that the Nash equilibrium is (do-not-bundle, do-not-bundle).

(iii) When products in the first t pairs are complements, and the products in the other pairs are substitutes, we obtain the following payoff table:

<table><tr><td></td><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td>Bundle</td></tr><tr><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{l} \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right) + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \right), \\ \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i1} \right) + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i2} - v_{i1}}{2} \right) \end{array} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{l} \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right) + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \right), \\ \sum_{i=t+1}^{m} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i1} \right) + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i2} - v_{i1}}{2} \right) - \varepsilon \end{array} \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>Bundle</td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{l} \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right) + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \right) - \varepsilon, \\ \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right) + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \right) \end{array} \right)$ </td><td> $\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i2} \right) - \varepsilon, \right. \\ \left. \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \overline{v_i} - v_{i1} \right) - \varepsilon \right) \end{array} \right.$  if bundles substitute $\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i1} - v_{i2}}{2} \right) - \varepsilon, \right. \\ \left. \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( \frac{\overline{v_i}}{2} + \frac{v_{i2} - v_{i1}}{2} \right) - \varepsilon \right) \end{array} \right.$  if bundles complement</td></tr></table>

A comparison of the payoffs in the above table shows that (do-not-bundle, do-not-bundle) as well as (bundle, bundle) is an equilibrium. Comparing the profits under these two equilibria shows that (bundle, bundle) is Pareto-dominant.

## Proof for Proposition 5

Proof: Note that the first t competing pair are complements and the others are substitutes. The following table shows the profit matrix for sellers.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td>Bundle</td></tr><tr><td>Do-not-bundle</td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \frac{(3v_i - \overline{v_i})(\overline{v_i} - v_i)}{4v_i} + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{9}, \\ \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \frac{(3v_i - \overline{v_i})(\overline{v_i} - v_i)}{4v_i} + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{9} \end{array} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \frac{(3v_i - \overline{v_i})(\overline{v_i} - v_i)}{4v_i} + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{9}, \\ \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \frac{(3v_i - \overline{ v_i})(\overline{ v_i} - v_i)}{4v_i} + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{9} - \varepsilon \end{array} \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>Bundle</td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \frac{(3v_i - \overline{v_i})(\overline{v_i} - v_i)}{4v_i} + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{9} - \varepsilon, \\ \sum_{i=t+1}^{n} \frac{(3v_i - \overline{v_i})(\overline{v_i} - v_i)}{4v_i} + \sum_{i=1}^{t} \frac{\overline{v_i}}{9} \end{array} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{c} \frac{n \sum_{i=1}^{n} (\overline{v_i} - v_i)}{2} - \varepsilon, \frac{n \sum_{i=1}^{n} (\overline{v_i} - v_i)}{2} - \varepsilon \\ \frac{n \sum_{i=1}^{n} \overline{v_i}}{4} - \varepsilon, \frac{n \sum_{i=1}^{n} \overline{v_i}}{4} - \varepsilon \end{array} \right)$  if bundles substitute</td></tr></table>

A comparison of the payoffs in the above table yields the result given in the proposition.

## Appendix B

# Complementary and Substitution of Information for a Linear Predictive Model

Consider the following linear predictive model used by a decision maker to estimate quantity $y .$

$$
y = \beta_ {1} x _ {1} + \beta_ {2} x _ {2}
$$

Assume the following prior distributions of predictor variables:

$$
x _ {i} \square N (\overline {{x _ {i}}}, \sigma_ {i} ^ {2})
$$

where $N ( \mu , \sigma ^ { 2 } )$ denotes a normal distribution with mean ${ } . \mu$ and variance $\sigma ^ { 2 } .$ Further, let the correlation coefficient between $x _ { 1 }$ and $x _ { 2 }$ be $\rho .$

The quality s of estimate $\bar { y }$ to the decision maker is a linearly declining function of its variance (or mean squared error) given by the following:

$$
x = 1 - \operatorname{var} (\bar {y})
$$

Let the quality of estimate of y when variable i alone is obtained be denoted as s , the quality of estimate of y when both variables are obtained $s _ { i } ,$ be denoted as $s _ { 1 + 2 } ,$ and the quality of estimate of y when neither variable is obtained be denoted as $s _ { 0 } .$

Further, denote the value of obtaining variable i alone as $\nu _ { i }$ and the values of obtaining both variables as $\nu _ { 1 + 2 } ,$ and let the value of obtaining a variable (or both) be proportional to the improvement in quality of the estimate compared to when no variable is obtained. That ${ \mathrm { i } } \mathbf { s } ,$

$$
\begin{array}{l} v _ {1} = k s _ {1} - k s _ {0}, \\ v _ {2} = k s _ {2} - k s _ {0}, \text { and } \\ v _ {1 + 2} = k s _ {1 + 2} - k s _ {0} \end{array}
$$

Using basic variance calculations, and using the fact that $\operatorname { v a r } ( x _ { i } | x _ { j } ) = ( 1 - \rho ^ { 2 } ) \sigma _ { i } ^ { 2 } ;$ , we can compute the value of the estimate under different scenarios as the following:

$$
\begin{array}{l} v _ {1} = k (\beta_ {1} ^ {2} \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} + \rho^ {2} \beta_ {2} ^ {2} \sigma_ {2} ^ {2} + 2 \beta_ {1} \beta_ {2} \rho \sigma_ {1} \sigma_ {2}), \\ v _ {2} = k (\beta_ {2} ^ {2} \sigma_ {2} ^ {2} + \rho^ {2} \beta_ {1} ^ {2} \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} + 2 \beta_ {1} \beta_ {2} \rho \sigma_ {1} \sigma_ {2}), \text { and } \\ v _ {1 + 2} = k (\beta_ {1} ^ {2} \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} + \beta_ {2} ^ {2} \sigma_ {2} ^ {2} + 2 \beta_ {1} \beta_ {2} \rho \sigma_ {1} \sigma_ {2}) \end{array}
$$

Comparing $\nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 }$ with $\nu _ { 1 + 2 } ,$ we find that $\nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } < \nu _ { 1 + 2 } \ .$ if and only if $\frac { - 2 \beta _ { 1 } \beta _ { 2 } \sigma _ { 1 } \sigma _ { 2 } } { \beta _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } \sigma _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } + \beta _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \sigma _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } } < \rho < 0$

## Appendix C

## Analysis of the Three-Product Case

There are two sellers, and each sells three products. Product i , i 0 {1, 2, 3} from each seller forms a competing pair. The two products in each competing pair are symmetric regarding their quality. The consumer utility from product i is $\nu _ { i }$ when i is bought from a single seller, and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$ when i from both sellers are bought. The products in a competing pair are complementors if $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } > 2 \nu _ { i }$ and substitutors if $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } \leq 2 \nu _ { i }$ Hereafter, we refer to competing pair i as complementor (substitutor) pair if the products in that pair are complementors (substitutors). As in the two-product case, we assume that if a seller chooses to offer her products as a bundle, then she incurs a small bundling cost $\varepsilon > 0$ . The bundling cost is the same regardless of the number of products bundled.

We have the following four possible scenarios depending on how many among the competing pairs are substitutors/complementors:

(a) All competing pairs are substitutors

(b) All competing pairs are complementors

(c) One pair is substitutor and the other two are complementors

(d) One pair is complementor and the other two are substitutors

Without loss of generality, we assume that competing pair 1 is the substitutor in scenario (c) and the complementor in scenario (d).

Regardless of the scenario, each seller has the following bundling/unbundling strategies in each:

A. Sell all products as a single bundle

B. Sell each product separately

C. Sell products 1 and 2 as a bundle, and sell product 3 separately

D. Sell products 1 and 3 as a bundle, and sell product 2 separately

E. Sell products 2 and 3 as a bundle, and sell product 1 separately

Now, we derive the sub game perfect equilibrium for each of the four scenarios (a) – (d). We denote the bundling strategies chosen by seller in the first stage using the notation (x, y) where x denotes the strategy of the first seller, y denotes the strategy chosen by the second seller, and both x and y come from the set of strategies {A, B, C, D, E} discussed above.

In the following proof, we denote the bundle of all three products as (1 + 2 + 3), the bundle of product 1 and product 2 as (1 + 2), the bundle of product 2 and product 3 as (2 + 3), and the bundle of product 1 and product 3 as (1 + 3).

## (a) All Competing Pairs Are Substitutors

Price equilibrium in second stage

1. Stage 1 strategy is (A, A). The competing bundles from the two sellers are substitutors (i.e., $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ . Using Proposition 3(b), we have price for each bundle as $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$

2. Stage 1 strategy is (A, B). Using Proposition 3(c), we have $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) , p _ { i 2 } = \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr )$

3. Stage 1 strategy is (A, C). The feasible and non-dominated regions for product prices result in the following constraints:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i}
$$

$$
v _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq v _ {3}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i}
$$

In each of the above inequalities, the left hand side and the right hand side represent, respectively the minimum value and the maximum value the consumer obtains from the product (or bundle if two or more products are offered as a bundle). In this scenario, we further note that lower bound of any inequality also represents the maximum value realized by the consumer if she buys the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle). The upper bound is the maximum value realized by the consumer if she does not buy the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle).

In the equilibrium, both sellers will set prices so that all products are sold. This can be proved by contradiction. Assume that a product offered by a seller is not sold in the equilibrium. The seller of this product can profitably set the price of this product to the lower bound of the feasible region for that product, shown in the corresponding inequality above, and the consumer will purchase the product.

When every product is sold, the following constraints should be satisfied.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \geq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \\ \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \geq p _ {3 2} \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \geq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \end{array}
$$

Comparing the two sets of constraints for prices, we get the following unique equilibrium prices.

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right), p _ {(1 + 2) 2} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right), p _ {3 2} = \left(\overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3}\right)
$$

4. Stage 1 strategy is (A, D). The proof is similar to the (A, C) case—the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 3 with product 1 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in the (A, C) case. Applying the same logic as in the $( \mathsf { A } , \mathsf { C } )$ case, we can show

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right), p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = \left(\overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1}\right) + \left(\overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3}\right), p _ {2 2} = \left(\overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2}\right)
$$

5. Stage 1 strategy is (A, E). Again, the proof is similar to the (A, C) case—the only difference is that seller 2 bundles products 2 and 3 in this case, whereas products 1 and 2 were bundled in the (A, C) case. Applying the same logic as in the (A, C) case, we can show

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right), p _ {(2 + 3) 2} = \left(\overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2}\right) + \left(\overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3}\right), p _ {1 2} = \left(\overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1}\right)
$$

6. Stage 1 strategy is (B, B). Using Proposition 3(a), we have each seller’s price for product i as $\overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i }$

7. Stage 1 strategy is (B, C). Since product 3 is sold separately by each seller, product 3 from the two sellers forms a competing pair. Using Proposition 3(a), we have $p _ { 3 1 } = p _ { 3 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 }$

For products 1 and 2, the case reduces to the two-product case in which both products are substitutes and, one sells the two products as a bundle and the other sells them separately. Using Proposition 1(c), we have the following result:

$$
p _ {i 1} = \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \forall i \in \{1, 2 \}, p _ {(1 + 2) 2} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

8. Stage 1 strategy is (B, D). The proof is similar to the (B, C) case—the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 3 with product 1 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in the (B, C) case. Applying the same logic as in the (B, C) case, we can show the following result:

$$
p _ {2 1} = p _ {2 2} = \bar {v} _ {2} - v _ {2} p _ {i 1} = (\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}) \forall i \in \{1, 3 \}, p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = (\bar {v} _ {1} - v _ {1}) + (\bar {v} _ {3} - v _ {3})
$$

9. Stage 1 strategy is (B, E). Again, the proof is similar to the (B, C) case—the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 2 with product 3 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in the (B, C) case. Applying the same logic as in the (B, C) case, we can show the following result:

$$
p _ {1 1} = p _ {2 2} = \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} p _ {i 1} = (\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}) \forall i \in \{2, 3 \}, p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = (\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2}) + (\overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3})
$$

10. Stage 1 strategy is (C, C). Since product 3 is sold separately by each seller, product 3 from the two sellers forms a competing pair. Using Proposition 1(a), we have $p _ { 3 1 } = p _ { 3 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 }$

For the bundles with products 1 and 2, the case reduces to the two-product bundle case in which the bundles are substitutes and, both sellers bundle. Again, using Proposition 1(c), we have the following result:

$$
p _ {(1 + 2) 2} = p _ {1 + 2 \_ 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

11. Stage 1 strategy is (C, D). The feasible and non-dominated regions for product prices result in the following constraints.

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i}
$$

$$
v _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i}
$$

$$
\overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq v _ {2}
$$

In each of the above inequalities, the left hand side and the right hand side represent, respectively the minimum value and the maximum value the consumer obtains from the product (or bundle if two or more products are offered as a bundle). In this scenario, we further note that lower bound of any inequality also represents the maximum value realized by the consumer if she buys the competing product (o competing products of those in the bundle). The upper bound is the maximum value realized by the consumer if she does not buy the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle).

In the equilibrium, both sellers will set prices so that all products are sold. This is can be proved by contradiction. Assume that a product offered by a seller is not sold in the equilibrium. The seller of this product can profitably set the price of this product to the lower bound of the feasible region for that product, shown in the corresponding inequality above, and the consumer will purchase the product.

When every product is sold, the following constraints should be satisfied.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \geq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \\ \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \geq p _ {3 1} \end{array}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \geq p _ {(1 + 3) 2}
$$

$$
v _ {2} - v _ {2} \geq p _ {2 2}
$$

Comparing the two sets of constraints for prices, we get the following unique equilibrium prices.

$$
\begin{array}{l} p _ {(1 + 2) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \\ p _ {3 1} = \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \\ p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \\ p _ {2 2} = \overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2} \end{array}
$$

12. Stage 1 strategy is (C, E). This case is similar to (C, D). Applying the same logic as that for (C, D) we get the following equilibrium prices:

$$
\begin{array}{l} p _ {(1 + 2) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \\ p _ {3 1} = \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \\ p _ {(2 + 3) 2} = \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \\ p _ {1 2} = \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

13. Stage 1 strategy is (D, D). This case is similar to (C, C). Applying the same logic as that for (C, C), we get $p _ { 2 1 } = p _ { 2 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 }$ $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } = p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } = \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) + \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right)$

14. Stage 1 strategy is (D, E). Again, this case is similar to (C, D) except the products that are bundled are different in the two cases. Applying the same logic as that for (C, D) we get the following equilibrium prices:

$$
\begin{array}{l} p _ {(1 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \\ p _ {2 1} = \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \\ p _ {(2 + 3) 2} = \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \\ p _ {1 2} = \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

15. Stage 1 strategy pair is (E, E). This case is similar to (C, C). Applying the same logic as that for (C, C), we get $p _ { 1 1 } = p _ { 1 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 }$ $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 1 } = \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right) + \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right)$

We have the following payoff matrix for the product policy game in the first stage. In each cell, the first expression is the payoff for seller 1 and the second expression is the payoff for seller 2.

## (b) All Competing Pairs Are Complementors

Price equilibrium in second stage

1 Stage 1 strategy is (A, A)

The competing bundles from the two sellers are complementors, i.e., $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } )$

Using Proposition 3(b) , we have price for each bundle as $\frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu _ { i } } }$

2. Stage 1 strategy is (A, B)

Using Proposition 3(c), we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } p _ { i 2 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu _ { i } } }$

## 3. Stage 1 strategy is (A, C)

The feasible and non-dominated regions for product prices result in the following constraints.

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

In each of the above inequalities, the left hand side and the right hand side represent, respectively the minimum value and the maximum value the consumer obtains from the product (or bundle if two or more products are offered as a bundle). In this scenario, we further note that lower bound of any inequality also represents the maximum value realized by the consumer if she buys only this product and not the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle). The upper bound is the maximum value realized by the consumer if she also buys the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle).

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following constraints should be satisfied.

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} + p _ {3 2} + p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

In the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { ( 1 \mathrm { ~ + ~ } 2 \mathrm { ~ + ~ } 3 ) 1 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { t } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right)$ and $p _ { 3 2 } + p _ { ( 1 \mathrm { ~ + ~ } 2 ) 2 }$ maximizes $\Biggl ( p _ { 3 2 } + p _ { 1 + 2 ) 2 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \Biggr ) \Biggl ( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - p _ { 3 2 } - p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \Biggr )$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 _ { - } 1 } = p _ { 3 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$

## 4. Stage 1 strategy is (A, D)

The proof is similar to the $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { C } )$ case; the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 3 with product 1 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in (A,C) case. Applying the same logic as in the (A,C) case, we can show the following:

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = p _ {2 2} + p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

## 5. Stage 1 strategy is (A, E)

Again, the proof is similar to the (A, C) case; the only difference is that seller 2 bundles products 2 and 3 in this case, whereas products 1 and 2 were bundled in (A,C) case. Applying the same logic as in the $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { C } )$ case, we can show the following:

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = p _ {1 2} + p _ {(2 + 3) 2} = \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

## 6. Stage 1 strategy is (B, B)

Using Proposition 3 (a), we have each seller’s price for product i as $\frac { \bar { \nu } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

## 7. Stage 1 strategy is (B, C)

Since product 3 is sold separately by each seller, product 3 from the two sellers form a competing pair. Since they are complements, using Proposition 1(a), we have $p _ { 3 1 } = p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { \bar { \nu } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

For products 1 and 2, the case reduces to the two-product case in which both products are complements and, one sells the two products as a bundle and the other sells them separately. Again, using Proposition 1(c), we have the following result:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} p _ {i 1} = p _ {(1 + 2) 2} = \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

## 8. Stage 1 strategy is (B, D)

The proof is similar to the (B, C) case; the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 3 with product 1 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in (B,C) case. Applying the same logic as in the (B,C) case, we can show the following result:

$$
p _ {2 1} = p _ {2 2} = \frac {\overline {{v _ {2}}}}{2}, \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} p _ {i 1} = p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

## 9. Stage 1 strategy is (B, E)

Again, the proof is similar to the (B, C) case; the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 2 with product 3 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in (B,C) case. Applying the same logic as in the (B,C) case, we can show the following result:

$$
p _ {1 1} = p _ {1 2} = \frac {\overline {{v _ {1}}}}{2}, \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} p _ {i 1} = p _ {(2 + 3) 2} = \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

## 10. Stage 1 strategy is (C, C)

Since product 3 is sold separately by each seller, product 3 from the two sellers form a competing pair. Using Proposition 1(a), we have $p _ { 3 1 } = p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { \bar { \nu } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

For the bundles with products 1 and 2, the case reduces to the two-product bundle case in which the bundles are complementors and, both sellers bundle. Again, using Proposition 1(b), we have the following result $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } = p _ { ( 1 _ { - } 2 ) 1 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i }$

## 11. Stage 1 strategy pair is (C, D)

The feasible and non-dominated regions for product prices result in the following constraints.

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2}
$$

In each of the above inequalities, the left hand side and the right hand side represent, respectively the minimum value and the maximum value the consumer obtains from the product (or bundle if two or more products are offered as a bundle). In this scenario, we further note that lower bound of any inequality also represents the maximum value realized by the consumer if she buys only this product and not the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle). The upper bound is the maximum value realized by the consumer if she also buys the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle).

In the equilibrium, both sellers will set prices so that all products are sold. This is can be proved by contradiction. Assume that a product offered by a seller is not sold in the equilibrium. The seller of this product can profitably set the price of this product to the lower bound of the feasible region for that product, shown in the corresponding inequality above, and the consumer will purchase the product.

When every product is sold, the following constraints should be satisfied.

$$
p _ {(1 + 2) 1} + p _ {3 1} + p _ {2 2} + p _ {(1 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed in the proof for the strategy (A, C), we obtain

$$
p _ {(1 + 2) 1} + p _ {3 1} = p _ {2 2} + p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

12. Stage 1 strategy is (C, E)

This case is similar to (C, D). Applying the same logic as that for (C,D) we get the following equilibrium prices.

$$
p _ {(1 + 2) 1} + p _ {3 1} = p _ {1 2} + p _ {(2 + 3) 2} = \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

## 13. Stage 1 strategy is (D, D)

This case is similar to (C, C). Applying the same logic as that for (C, C), we get $p _ { 2 1 } = p _ { 2 2 } = \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } } { 2 } , p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } = p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } \ .$

14. Stage 1 strategy is (D, E)

Again, this case is similar to (C, D) except the products that are bundled are different in the two cases. Applying the same logic as that for (C, D) we get the following equilibrium prices.

$$
p _ {(1 + 3) 1} + p _ {2 1} = p _ {1 2} + p _ {(2 + 3) 2} = \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \overline {{v _ {i}}}
$$

## 15. Stage 1 strategy is (E, E)

This case is similar to (C, C). Applying the same logic as that for (C, C), we get $p _ { 1 1 } = p _ { 1 2 } = \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } } { 2 } , p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 1 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i }$

The price equilibrium for other 10 possible strategy pairs can be determined from the above using symmetry.

We have the following payoff matrix for the product policy game in the first stage. In each cell, the first expression is the payoff for seller 1 and the second expression is the payoff for seller 2.
