## (c) One Pair Is Substitutor and the Other Two Are Complementors

Without loss of generality, we assume that competing pair 1 is the substitutor and the other two pairs are complementors. That is, $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } \leq 2 \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \nu _ { 2 }$ , and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \nu _ { 3 }$

## Price equilibrium in second stage

## 1. Stage 1 strategy is (A, A)

We need to consider two cases: (i) the competing bundles are substitutors $( \mathrm { i . e . , ~ } \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big ) )$ ), and (ii) the competing bundles are complementors (i.e., $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big ) \ ,$ ).

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

Using Proposition 3 (b) for the case of competing substitutors, we have price for each bundle as $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$

(ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

Using Proposition 3 (b) for the case of competing complementors, we have price for each bundle as $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } } { 2 }$

## 2. Stage 1 strategy is (A, B)

The feasible and non-dominated regions for seller 1’s product price depends on whether $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , or $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ is satisfied.

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \\ \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq v _ {1} \\ v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2} \\ v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \end{array}
$$

In each of the above inequalities, the left hand side and the right hand side represent, respectively the minimum value and the maximum value the consumer obtains from the product (or bundle if two or more products are offered as a bundle). In the first and second inequalities, the lower bound of any inequality also represents the maximum value realized by the consumer if she buys the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle), and the upper bound is the maximum value realized by the consumer if she does not buy the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle). In the other inequalities, lower bound of the inequality also represents the maximum value realized by the consumer if she buys only this product and not the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle), and the upper bound is the maximum value realized by the consumer if she also buys the competing product (or competing products of those in the bundle).

In the equilibrium, both sellers will set prices so that all products are sold. This is can be proved by contradiction. Assume that a product offered by a seller is not sold in the equilibrium. The seller of this product can profitably set the price of this product to the lower bound of the feasible region for that product, shown in the corresponding inequality above, and the consumer will purchase the product

When every product is sold, for $p _ { 1 2 } ,$ the following constraint should be satisfied: $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } = p _ { 1 2 }$ . Furthermore, for seller 1, the value of product 1 in the bundle will be equal to $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 }$ . Therefore, the following constraint should be satisfied for $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 }$ :

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} v _ {i} + \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) + \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1}
$$

In the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } - p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right)$ and ${ p _ { 3 2 } + p _ { 2 2 } }$ maximizes $\displaystyle \left( p _ { 3 2 } + p _ { 2 2 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } - p _ { 3 2 } + p _ { 2 2 } - \left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { i } \right) \right)$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 1 } = p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i }$

## (ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \\ \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq v _ {1} \\ v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2} \\ v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \end{array}
$$

In the equilibrium, both sellers will set prices so that all products are sold.

When every product is sold, for $p _ { 1 2 } ,$ the following constraints should be satisfied: $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } = p _ { 1 2 }$ . Furthermore, for seller 1, the value of product 1 in the bundle will be equal to $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 }$ . Therefore, the following constraint should be satisfied for $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } .$

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} v _ {i} + \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) + \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1}
$$

In the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } - p _ { 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right)$ and ${ p _ { 3 2 } } + { p _ { 2 2 } }$ maximizes $\displaystyle { \left( p _ { 3 2 } + p _ { 2 2 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } - p _ { 3 2 } + p _ { 2 2 } - \left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) \right) }$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 1 } = p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i }$

## 3. Stage 1 strategy is (A, C)

For seller 1, we need to consider two cases: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and (ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ . For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated regions fo ${ \bf \gamma } _ { p _ { 3 2 } }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { _ 3 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { _ 3 } - \nu _ { _ 3 } \right)$ , and the feasible and non-dominated region fo $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 }$ depends on whether $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ or $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ . So, the price equilibrium depends on wh ich of the following conditions are satisfied:

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

(ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

(iii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big ) \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

Note that $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ is not possible because $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } \leq 2 \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \nu _ { 2 }$ and , $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \nu _ { 3 }$ in this scenario.

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i}
$$

$$
v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq v _ {3} - v _ {3}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 2) 2}
$$

$$
v _ {3} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed for the strategy (A, B), we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) = p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big ) \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i}
$$

$$
v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 2) 2}
$$

$$
v _ {3} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed for the strategy (A, B), we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) = p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

(iii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed for the strategy $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { B } )$ , we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } = p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } + p _ { 3 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } } { 2 }$

## 4. Stage 1 strategy is (A, D)

The proof is similar to the $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { C } )$ case; the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 3 with product 1 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { C } )$ case. Applying the same logic as in the $( \mathsf { A } , \mathsf { C } )$ case, we can show that there are three possible combinations of conditions to consider, and that the equilibrium prices can be shown to be the following:

$$
\text {(i)} \quad \overline {{v}} _ {1} + \overline {{v}} _ {2} + \overline {{v}} _ {3} \leq 2 (v _ {1} + v _ {2} + v _ {3}) \text { and } \overline {{v}} _ {1} + \overline {{v}} _ {2} \leq 2 (v _ {1} + v _ {2})
$$

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 3) 2}
$$

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {2 2} = \frac {v _ {2}}{2}
$$

(ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 3) 2}
$$

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {2 2} = \frac {v _ {2}}{2}
$$

(iii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ 0

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = p _ {(1 + 3) 2} + p _ {2 2} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \frac {\overline {{v _ {i}}}}{2}
$$

## 5. Stage 1 strategy is (A, E)

For seller 1, we need to consider two cases: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , and (ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { 1 2 }$ is given by $\left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } , \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ , and the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ is given by $\left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } , \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) \right)$

So, the price equilibrium depends on which of the following conditions are satisfied:

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

(ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big ) .$

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
v _ {1} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq v _ {1}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} = p _ {1 2} \\ \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \overline {{v _ {1}}} + v _ {1} \leq \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \end{array}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed for the strategy $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { B } )$ , we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 1 } = \sum _ { i - 2 } ^ { 3 } \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } } { 2 }$

(ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} (\bar {v} - v _ {i})
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq v _ {1}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} = p _ {1 2} \\ \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \overline {{v _ {1}}} + v _ {1} \leq \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \end{array}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed for the strategy (A, B), we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 1 } = p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i - 2 } ^ { 3 } \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } } { 2 }$

## 6. Stage 1 strategy is (B, B)

Using Proposition 3 (a) , we have each seller’s price for product 1 as $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 }$ , for product 2 as $\frac { \bar { \nu } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$ , and for product 3 as $\frac { \bar { \nu } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

## 7. Stage 1 strategy is (B, C)

For seller 1, the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { 1 1 }$ is given by $\left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } , \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ , that for $p _ { 2 1 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { 2 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ , and that for ${ \bf \dot { \rho } } _ { p _ { 3 2 } }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { 3 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { 3 2 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { 3 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 }$ depends on whether $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ or $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

So, the price equilibrium depends on which of the following conditions are satisfied:

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { l } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { l } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 1} \leq v _ {1} \\ v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 1} \leq \overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2} \\ v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \\ v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} = p _ {1 1} \\ v _ {2} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} - \overline {{v _ {1}}} + v _ {1} \leq \overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2} \end{array}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed for the strategy $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { B } )$ , we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } - \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 1 } = p _ { 2 1 } = \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$ and $p _ { 3 2 } = p _ { 3 1 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{v _ {1}}} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 1} \leq v _ {1} \\ v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 1} \leq \overline {{v _ {2}}} - v _ {2} \\ v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v _ {i}}} - v _ {i}\right) \\ v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v _ {3}}} - v _ {3} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed for the strategy (A, B), we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } - \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 1 } = p _ { 2 1 } = \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$ and $p _ { 3 2 } = p _ { 3 1 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

## 8. Stage 1 strategy is (B, D)

The proof is similar to the (B, C) case – the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 3 with product 1 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in (B,C) case. Applying the same logic as in the (B,C) case, we can show the following result:

$$

For the bundles with products 1 and 2, the case reduces to the two-product bundle case. The bundles can be substitutes or complements. $\mathrm { S 0 } { } .$ using Proposition 1(b), we have the following result:

## 11. Stage 1 strategy is (C, D)

For seller 1, the feasible and non-dominated region for ${ p } _ { 3 1 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { _ 3 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { _ 3 } - \nu _ { _ 3 } \right)$ . $$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$

$$

Given the above prices for the bundles, ${ p } _ { 3 1 } + { p } _ { 2 2 }$ cannot exceed $2 \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 }$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products.

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraints $\nu _ { 3 } \leq p _ { 3 1 } \leq \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 2 } \leq p _ { 2 2 } \leq \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 }$ are more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 2 2 } .$ . Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { 3 1 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { 3 1 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) \left( 2 \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - p _ { 3 1 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ and $p _ { 2 2 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { 2 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \big ) \big ( 2 \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - p _ { 2 2 } - \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { 3 1 } = \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } - \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } } { 2 } \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } p _ { 2 2 } = \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } - \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium.

$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

Given the above price, $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \left\{ 1 , 3 \right\} } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products.

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraints $\nu _ { 3 } \leq p _ { 3 1 } \leq \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 2 } \leq p _ { 2 2 } \leq \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } , \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } \leq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \bigl ( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr )$ ar e more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ . Therefore, $p _ { 3 1 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , p _ { 2 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } , p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$

If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , we have $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \left( \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ . Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { 3 1 } \quad + \quad p _ { ( 1 } \quad + \quad 2 ) 1$ maximizes $\left( p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \bigl ( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) - p _ { 3 1 } - p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ and $p _ { 2 2 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { 2 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \big ) \Bigg ( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \big ( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) - p _ { 2 2 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \Bigg )$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } + { \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } } { 2 } } { \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } } p _ { 2 2 } = { \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } } { 2 } } .$

(iii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$

Given the above price, $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products. I $\mathrm { ~ f ~ } \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraints $\nu _ { 3 } \leq p _ { 3 1 } \leq \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 2 } \leq p _ { 2 2 } \leq \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } , \ \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } ( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } )$ are more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } .$ . Therefore, $p _ { 3 1 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , p _ { 2 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } , p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , we have $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ . Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { 3 1 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { 3 1 } - \nu _ { 3 } \big ) \bigg ( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) - p _ { 3 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \bigg )$ and $p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { 2 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - p _ { 2 2 } - p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . (iv) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ 1

## 12. Stage 1 strategy is (C, E)

For seller 1, the feasible and non-dominated region for ${ \bf \dot { \rho } } _ { p _ { 3 1 } }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { _ 3 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { _ 3 } - \nu _ { _ 3 } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated region $\mathrm { f o r } p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ depends on whether $2 \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \nu _ { i } } \le \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } } \mathrm { o r } 2 \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \nu _ { i } } > \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } }$ . For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { 1 2 }$ given by $\left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } , \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ is given by $\left( \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } { \nu _ { i } , \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } ) } \right)$

So, the price equilibrium depends on which of the following conditions are satisfied:

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \big (\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \big) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \\ \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} \big (\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \big) \\ v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \\ v _ {1} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} = p _ {1 2} \end{array}
$$

Given the above prices, $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) - \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \big )$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraints $\sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ are more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } .$ Therefore, $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \bigl ( \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) , p _ { 3 1 } = \bar { \nu } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 }$

If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , we have $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \big )$ . Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { 3 1 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { 3 1 } - \nu _ { 3 } \big ) \bigg ( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) - p _ { 3 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \bigg )$ and $p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) - p _ { 1 2 } - p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { \bar { \nu } _ { 3 } + \nu _ { 1 } - \bar { \nu } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$ and $p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } + 3 \nu _ { 2 } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \\ \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \\ v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \\ v _ {1} - v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} = p _ {1 2}
$$

Given the above price, $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products.

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraints $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } \leq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , \nu _ { 3 } \leq p _ { 3 1 } \leq \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 }$ are more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ Therefore, $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big )$ $p _ { 3 1 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 }$

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , we have $p _ { 3 1 } + p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ . Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { 3 1 }$ maximizes $\big ( p _ { 3 1 } - \nu _ { 3 } \big ) \bigg ( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) - \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \big ) - p _ { 3 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \bigg )$ and $p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) - p _ { 1 2 } - p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { _ { 3 2 } } + p _ { _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } + 2 \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } } { 2 }$ and $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = { \frac { { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } + { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } } { 2 } }$

## 13. Stage 1 strategy is (D, D)

This case is similar to (C, C). Since product 2 is sold separately by each seller, product 2 from the two sellers form a competing pair. Since they are complements, using Proposition 1(a), we have $p _ { 2 1 } = p _ { 2 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$

For the bundles with products 1 and 3, the case reduces to the two-product bundle case. The bundles can be substitutes or complements. So, using Proposition 1(b), we have the following result:

$$
\begin{array}{l} p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = p _ {(1 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) i f 2 \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \overline {{v}} _ {i} \\ p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = p _ {(1 + 3) 1} = \frac {\sum_ {i \in \{1 , 3 \}} \overline {{v}} _ {i}}{2} i f 2 \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} > \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \overline {{v}} _ {i} \end{array}
$$

## 14. Stage 1 strategy is (D, E)

Again, this case is similar to (C,E) except the products bundled by 1 are different in the two cases. Applying the same logic as that for (C,E) we get the following equilibrium prices.

(a) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 3) 1}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} = p _ {1 2}
$$

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraints $\sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \bigl ( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) , \nu _ { 3 } \leq p _ { 2 1 } \leq \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 }$ are more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } .$ . Therefore, $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } ) , p _ { 3 1 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 }$

If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , we have $p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) - \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \bigr )$ . Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { 2 1 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) - p _ { 1 2 } - p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ and $p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) - p _ { 1 2 } - p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { 2 1 } = { \frac { { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } - { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 1 } } { 2 } } { \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } } p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = { \frac { { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + 3 \nu _ { 1 } + 2 \nu _ { 3 } - { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } } { 2 } }$ .

(b) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

$$
\bar {v} _ {1} - v _ {1} = p _ {1 2}
$$

Given the above price, $p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products. If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , , the constraints $\sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } ) , \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } ) , \nu _ { 2 } \leq p _ { 2 1 } \leq \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 3 }$ are more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } .$ Therefore, $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , p _ { 2 1 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 }$ If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , we have $p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } + p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ . Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, ${ p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) - p _ { 2 1 } - p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right)$ and $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right) - p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right)$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { 2 1 } + p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 1 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } + 2 \nu _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$ and $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$

## 15. Stage 1 strategy is (E, E)

This case is similar to $( \mathbf { C } , \mathbf { C } ) \mathrm { o r } \left( \mathbf { D } , \mathbf { D } \right)$ . Applying the same logic as that for $\textstyle ( \mathbf { C } , \mathbf { C } )$ , we get $p _ { 1 1 } = p _ { 1 2 } = \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } , p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 } = p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 1 } = \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$ The price equilibrium for other 10 possible strategy pairs can be determined from the above using symmetry.

## Bundling Equilibrium in the First Stage

The analysis of the second stage shows that the price equilibrium differs depending on the relative product valuation parameters. Specifically, the price equilibrium depends on the combination of the following conditions:

$$
\text {(i)} \quad \overline {{v}} _ {1} + \overline {{v}} _ {2} + \overline {{v}} _ {3} > 2 (v _ {1} + v _ {2} + v _ {3}) \text { or } \overline {{v}} _ {1} + \overline {{v}} _ {2} + \overline {{v}} _ {3} \leq (v _ {1} + v _ {2} + v _ {3})
$$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big ) \mathrm { o r } \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq \left( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \right)$

(iii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big ) \mathrm { ~ o r ~ } \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

Since the payoff matrix for stage 1 analysis depends on the combination, and there are numerous combinations, we show the stage 1 analysis for one of the combinations. The analysis for other combinations is similar to the one presented, and the results for those combinations are qualitatively similar to the one presented here.

$$
\text { Assume } \bar {v} _ {1} + \bar {v} _ {2} + \bar {v} _ {3} \leq (v _ {1} + v _ {2} + v _ {3}), \bar {v} _ {1} + \bar {v} _ {2} \leq (v _ {1} + v _ {2}), \text { and } \bar {v} _ {1} + \bar {v} _ {3} \leq (v _ {1} + v _ {3})
$$

We have the following payoff matrix for the product policy game in the first stage for the above combination. In each cell, the first expression is the payoff for seller 1 and the second expression is the payoff for seller 2.
