v_i) - \varkappa \end{array} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{c}\sum_{i=1}^{3} (\overline{v}_i - v_i) - \varepsilon, \\ \sum_{i=1}^{3} (\overline{v}_i - v_i) - \varepsilon \end{array} \right)$ </td></tr><tr><td>E</td><td> $\left( \begin{pmatrix} \overline{v}_1 - v_1) + \sum_{i \in \{2,3\}} \frac{\overline{v}_i}{2} - \varepsilon, \\ (\overline{v}_1 - v_1) + \sum_{i \in \{2,3\}} \frac{\overline{v}_i}{2} - \varepsilon \end{pmatrix}$ </td><td> $\left( \begin{pmatrix} \overline{v}_1 - v_1) + \sum_{i \in \{2,3\}} \frac{\overline{v}_i}{2}, \\ (\overline{v}_1 - v_1) + \sum_{i \in \{2,3\}} \frac{\overline{v}_i}{2} - \varepsilon \end{pmatrix}$ </td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{c}\sum_{i=1}^{3} (\overline{v}_i - v_i) - \varepsilon, \\ \sum_{i=1}^{3} (\overline{v}_i - v_i) - \varepsilon \end{array} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \begin{array}{c}\sum_{i=1}^{3} (\bar{v}_i - v_i) - \varepsilon, \\ \sum_{i=1}^{3} (\bar{v}_i - v_i) - \varepsilon \end{array} \right)$ </td><td> $\left( \begin{pmatrix} \overline{v}_1 - v_1) + \sum_{i=2}^{3} \frac{\overline{v}_i}{2} - \varepsilon, \\ (\overline{v}_1 - v_1) + \sum_{i=2}^{3} \frac{\overline{v}_i}{2} \end{pmatrix}$ </td></tr></table>

From the above payoff matrix, it is easy to see that (A, A) is a Pareto-optimal Nash equilibrium because $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } \leq 2 \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \nu _ { 2 }$ , and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \nu _ { 3 }$ We also have (C, D), (C, E), (D, C), (D, E), (E, C), and (E, D) as the other Nash equilibria that have the same equilibrium payoff as $( \operatorname { A } , \operatorname { A } ) .$ We note that all these equilibria involve both sellers bundling at least two products, and at least one seller bundling the substitutor product 1 with one of the other two complementor products.

## (d) One Pair Is Complementor and the Other Two Are Substitutors

Without loss of generality, we assume that competing pair 1 is the complementor and the other two pairs are substitutors. That is, $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } > 2 \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \nu _ { 2 }$ and , $\overline { { \nu } } _ { _ 3 } \leq 2 \nu _ { _ 3 }$

## Price Equilibrium in Second Stage

## 1. Stage 1 strategy is (A, A)

Same as $( \mathsf { A } , \mathsf { A } )$ for scenario (c) discussed previously.

## 2. Stage 1 strategy is (A, B)

For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { 1 2 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ , the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { 2 2 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } , \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ , and the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { 3 2 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 3 } \right)$

For seller 1, we need to consider two cases: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and (ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i}
$$

$$
v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq v _ {2}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} - \overline {{v}} _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq v _ {3}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 2}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 2}
$$

$$
v _ {1} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq \left(\bar {v} _ {1} - v _ {1}\right)
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed, we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) = p _ { 1 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$

(ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq v _ {2}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} - \overline {{v}} _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq v _ {3}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 2}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 2}
$$

$$
v _ {1} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq \left(\bar {v} _ {1} - v _ {1}\right)
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model discussed, we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) = p _ { 1 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$

## 3. Stage 1 strategy is $( \mathsf { A } , \mathsf { C } )$

For seller 1, we need to consider two cases: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and (ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { 3 2 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ , and the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 }$ depends on whether $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big ) \mathrm { ~ o r ~ } \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ . So, the price equilibrium depends on which of the following conditions are satisfied: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ , (ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ (iii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ . Note that the conditions $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ are not possible because $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \nu _ { 3 }$ in this scenario.

$$
\text {(i)} \quad \overline {{v}} _ {1} + \overline {{v}} _ {2} + \overline {{v}} _ {3} \leq 2 (v _ {1} + v _ {2} + v _ {3}) \text { and } \overline {{v}} _ {1} + \overline {{v}} _ {2} \geq 2 (v _ {1} + v _ {2})
$$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq v _ {3} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 2} \\ p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \end{array}
$$

(ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ 0

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 2) 2}
$$

$$
v _ {3} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model, we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) = p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

(iii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ 0

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model, we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } = p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } + p _ { 3 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } } { 2 }$

## 4. Stage 1 strategy is $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { D } )$

The proof is similar to the $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { C } )$ case; the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 3 with product 1 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in $( \mathsf { A } , \mathsf { C } )$ case. Applying the same logic as in the $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { C } )$ case, we can show that there are three possible combinations of conditions to consider, and that the equilibrium prices will be the following:

$$
\text {(i)} \quad \overline {{v}} _ {1} + \overline {{v}} _ {2} + \overline {{v}} _ {3} \leq 2 (v _ {1} + v _ {2} + v _ {3}) \text { and } \overline {{v}} _ {1} + \overline {{v}} _ {2} \leq 2 (v _ {1} + v _ {2})
$$

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 3) 2}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 2}
$$

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

(ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ 0

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 3) 2}
$$

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {2 2} = \frac {\bar {v} _ {2}}{2}
$$

(iii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = p _ {(1 + 2) 2} + p _ {3 2} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \frac {\overline {{v}} _ {i}}{2}
$$

## 5. Stage 1 strategy is $( \mathrm { A } , \mathrm { E } )$

For seller 1, we need to consider two cases: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , and (ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { 1 2 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { 1 } , \bar { \nu } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ , and the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ is given by $\left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \bigl ( \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) , \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right)$

So, the price equilibrium depends on which of the following conditions are satisfied: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , and (ii) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

(a) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} v _ {i}
$$

$$
v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(2 + 3) 2}
$$

$$
p _ {1 2} = \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1}
$$

(b) $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2 + 3) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} v _ {i}
$$

$$
v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(2 + 3) 2}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model , we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 + 3 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) = p _ { 1 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$

## 6. Stage 1 strategy is (B, B)

Using Proposition 3 (a) , we have each seller’s price for product 1 as $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 }$ , for product 2 as $\frac { \bar { \nu } _ { 2 } } { 2 }$ , and for product 3 as $\frac { \bar { \nu } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

## 7. Stage 1 strategy is (B, C)

For seller 1, the feasible and non-dominated region fo $p _ { 1 1 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ , that for p<sub>21</sub> is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } , \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ , and that for $p _ { 3 1 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { 3 2 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 }$ depends on whether $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ or $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \geq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

So, the price equilibrium depends on which of the following conditions are satisfied: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big ) , ( \mathrm { i i } ) ~ \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 1} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 1} \leq v _ {2} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3} \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq v _ {3} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 2} \\ v _ {1} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} - \overline {{v}} _ {2} + v _ {2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model, we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } - \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 2 } = p _ { 1 1 } = \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 1} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 1} \leq v _ {2}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 2} \leq v _ {3}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 2} \\ v _ {1} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 2} - \overline {{v}} _ {2} + v _ {2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model, we obtain $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 2 } - \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 2 } = p _ { 1 1 } = \frac { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$

## 8. Stage 1 strategy is (B, D)

The proof is similar to the (B, C) case ; the only difference is that seller 2 bundles product 3 with product 1 in this case, whereas it was product 2 that was bundled with product 1 in (B, C) case. Applying the same logic as in the (B, C) case, we can show the following result:

$$
\bar {v} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 1}, \bar {v} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1}, \bar {v} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {3 2} \text { and } p _ {(1 + 3) 2} - \bar {v} _ {3} + v _ {3} = p _ {1 1} = \frac {\bar {v} _ {1}}{2}.
$$

## 9. Stage 1 strategy is (B, E)

For seller 1, the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { 1 1 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ , that for $p _ { 2 1 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } , \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ , and that for $p _ { 1 2 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { 1 2 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated regions for $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ is given by $\left( \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } , \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { 3 } \nu _ { i } \right)$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 1} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 1} \leq v _ {2} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3} \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \\ v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 1}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i = 2} ^ {3} \overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} = p _ {(2 + 3) 2}
$$

Using the Nash Bargaining Solution model we obtain $p _ { 1 1 } = p _ { 2 1 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$

## 10. Stage 1 strategy is $\textstyle ( \mathbf { C } , \mathbf { C } )$

Since product 3 is sold separately by each seller, product 3 from the two sellers form a competing pair. Since they are complements, using Proposition 1(a), we have $p _ { 3 1 } = p _ { 3 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

For the bundles with products 1 and 2, the case reduces to the two-product bundle case. The bundles can be substitutes or complements. ${ \mathrm { S o } } ,$ using Proposition 1(b), we have the following result:

$$
p _ {(1 + 2) 2} = p _ {(1 + 2) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) i f 2 \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \bar {v} _ {i}
$$

## 11. Stage 1 strategy is (C, D)

For seller 1, the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { 3 1 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { _ 3 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { _ 3 } - \nu _ { _ 3 } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ depends on whether $2 \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \nu _ { i } } \le \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } } \mathrm { ~ o r ~ } 2 \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \nu _ { i } } > \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } }$ . For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated region fo $p _ { 2 2 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } , \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 }$ depends on if $2 \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } \ \mathrm { ~ o r ~ } \ 2 \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } > \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i }$

So, the price equilibrium depends on which of the following conditions are satisfied: (i) $\nu _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 1 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ (ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , (iii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , (iv) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

## (i) $\nu _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 1 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \\ \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq v _ {2} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 2) 1}
$$

$$
\sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 3) 2}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1}
$$

$$
\overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 2}
$$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i - 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \\ \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq v _ {2} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \big (\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \big) \leq p _ {(1 + 3) 2} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 2} \end{array}
$$

Given the above prices, $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) - \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \bigr ) - \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \bigr )$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products.

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraint $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \nu _ { i } } \le p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } \le \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ is more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $\cdot p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } .$ Therefore, $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , then $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr ) - \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \bigr ) - \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \bigr )$

(iii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \big (\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \big) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \\ \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \big (\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \big) \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq v _ {2} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 2} \end{array}
$$

Given the above prices, $\boldsymbol { { p } } _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) - \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \big ) - \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products.

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraint $\sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \bigl ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \bigr )$ is more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } .$ Therefore, $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i \in [ 1 , 3 ] } \left( { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$

If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , then $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right) .$

(iv) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$ and $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i - 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \\ \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} \leq p _ {2 2} \leq v _ {2} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {2} - v _ {2} = p _ {2 2} \end{array}
$$

Given the above prices, $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right)$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products.

If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraints $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } \leq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } \leq \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big )$ are more stringent than the nonnegative surplus constraint on $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ . Therefore, $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } = \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big )$

$$
\text {   If   } \bar {v} _ {1} + \bar {v} _ {2} + \bar {v} _ {3} > 2 (v _ {1} + v _ {2} + v _ {3}), \text {   then   } p _ {(1 + 3) 2} + p _ {(1 + 2) 1} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {3} \bar {v} _ {i} - (\bar {v} _ {3} - v _ {3}) - (\bar {v} _ {2} - v _ {2}).
$$

Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right) - p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \nu _ { i } \right)$ and $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \left( \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 2 } \right) - p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \right)$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { ( 1 + 3 ) 2 } = \nu _ { 3 } + \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$ and $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \nu _ { 2 } + \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } } { 2 }$

## 12. Stage 1 strategy is (C, E)

For seller 1, the feasible and non-dominated region for $\cdot p _ { 3 1 }$ is given by $\left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } , \nu _ { 3 } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ depends on whether $2 \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \nu _ { i } } \le \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } } \mathrm { o r } 2 \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \nu _ { i } } > \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } }$ . For seller 2, the feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { 1 2 }$ is given by $\left( \nu _ { i } , \bar { \nu _ { i } } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ . The feasible and non-dominated region for $p _ { ( 2 + 3 ) 2 }$ is given by $\left( \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } { \big ( } { \overline { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } { \big ) } , \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \nu _ { i } \right)$

So, the price equilibrium depends on which of the following conditions are satisfied: (i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big ) , ( \mathrm { i i } ) ~ \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

(i) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \\ \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3} \\ v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \\ \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1} \end{array}
$$

Given the above prices, $p _ { 1 2 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \bar { \nu } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \left( \bar { \nu } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products

If $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } \leq 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraint $\nu _ { { 1 } } \leq p _ { { 1 } { 2 } } \leq \overline { { \nu } } _ { { 1 } } - \nu _ { { 1 } }$ is more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { \mathrm { 1 l } }$ Therefore, $p _ { 1 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 }$

If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , then $p _ { 1 2 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \sum _ { i \in \left\{ 2 , 3 \right\} } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$

(ii) $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } \big )$

The following should hold in the equilibrium.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {2} v _ {i} \leq p _ {(1 + 2) 1} \leq \sum_ {i - 1} ^ {2} \big (\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \big) \\ \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} \big (\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i} \big) \leq p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} v _ {i} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} \leq p _ {3 1} \leq v _ {3} \\ v _ {1} \leq p _ {1 2} \leq \overline {{v}} _ {1} - v _ {1} \end{array}
$$

Since in the equilibrium, each seller will set prices so that every offering is sold, the following characterizes the equilibrium prices for each offering from each seller.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i \in \{2, 3 \}} \left(\overline {{v}} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) = p _ {(2 + 3) 2} \\ \overline {{v}} _ {3} - v _ {3} = p _ {3 1} \end{array}
$$

Given the above prices, $p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ cannot exceed $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \sum _ { i \in \left\{ 2 , 3 \right\} } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right)$ in order to ensure non-negative surplus for the consumer by buying these products.

If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , the constraints $\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } \leq p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } \leq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \ \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , \nu _ { 1 } \leq p _ { 1 2 } \leq \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 }$ are more stringent than the non-negative surplus constraint on $p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ . Therefore, $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \big ) , p _ { 1 2 } = \big ( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \big )$

If $\overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 1 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 2 } + \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } > 2 \big ( \nu _ { 1 } + \nu _ { 2 } + \nu _ { 3 } \big )$ , then $p _ { 1 2 } + p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \sum _ { i \in \left\{ 2 , 3 \right\} } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) .$

Therefore, in the Nash Bargaining Solution, $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 }$ maximizes $\left( p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } - \sum _ { i \in \{ 1 , 2 \} } \nu _ { i } \right) \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } \right) - \sum _ { i \in \{ 2 , 3 \} } \left( \overline { { \nu } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } \right) - p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } - \nu _ { 1 } \right)$ and $p _ { 1 2 }$ maximizes $\left( { p _ { 1 2 } - \nu _ { 1 } } \right) \left(  \\\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 3 } { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \left( { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { 3 } - \nu _ { 3 } } \right) } - \sum _ { i \in \left\{ 2 , 3 \right\} } { \left( { \overline { { { \nu } } } _ { i } - \nu _ { i } } \right) - p _ { 1 2 } - \sum _ { i \in \left\{ 1 , 2 \right\} } { \nu _ { i } } } \right)$ . Solving the above optimization problems simultaneously gives the following solution: $p _ { ( 1 + 2 ) 1 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + 2 \nu _ { 3 } + 2 \nu _ { 2 } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$ and $p _ { 1 2 } = \frac { \overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } + 2 \nu _ { 3 } - \overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } } { 2 }$

## 13. Stage 1 strategy is (D, D)

This case is similar to (C, C). Since product 2 is sold separately by each seller, product 2 from the two sellers form a competing pair. Since they are substitutes, using Proposition 1(a), we have $p _ { 2 1 } = p _ { 2 2 } = \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } - \nu _ { 1 }$

For the bundles with products 1 and 3, the case reduces to the two-product bundle case. The bundles can be substitutes or complements. $\mathrm { S 0 } { } .$ using Proposition 1(b), we have the following result:

$$
p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = p _ {(1 + 3) 1} = \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \left(\bar {v} _ {i} - v _ {i}\right) i f 2 \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} \leq \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \bar {v} _ {i}
$$

$$
p _ {(1 + 3) 2} = p _ {(1 + 3) 1} = \frac {\sum_ {i \in \{1 , 3 \}} \overline {{v}} _ {i}}{2} i f 2 \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} v _ {i} > \sum_ {i \in \{1, 3 \}} \overline {{v}} _ {i}
$$

From the above payoff matrix, it is easy to see that $( \mathsf { A } , \mathsf { A } )$ is a Paerto-optimal Nash equilibrium because $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 1 } \leq 2 \nu _ { 1 } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { 2 } > 2 \nu _ { 2 }$ and, $\overline { { \nu } } _ { 3 } > 2 \nu _ { 3 }$ We also have ( $\bar { \mathsf { A } } , \mathsf { C } ) , ( \mathsf { A } , \mathsf { D } ) , ( \mathsf { C } , \mathsf { A } ) , ( \mathsf { C } , \mathsf { D } ) , ( \mathsf { C } , \mathsf { E } ) , ( \mathsf { D } , \mathsf { A } ) , ( \mathsf { D } , \mathsf { C } ) , ( \bar { \mathsf { D } } , \mathsf { D } ) , \mathsf { \Omega } ( \mathsf { D } , \mathsf { E } ) , ( \mathsf { E } , \mathsf { C } ) , \mathrm { a n d \left( \mathsf { E } , \mathsf { D } \right) }$ as the other Nash equilibria that have the same equilibrium payoff as (A,A). We note that all these equilibria involve both sellers bundling at least two products, and at least one seller bundling the complementor product 1 with one of the other two complementor products.
